My father was a delivery man, who travelled to places to make sure he delivered things that mattered most to people on time, and in the process brought happiness into their lives. I have always dreamt of working for a logistics company to continue what my father started, bringing happiness to countless people.

TransPacific is an esteemed enterprise, and I am aware that to earn a seat as an intern, besides the intent, one should demonstrate an excellent education track record. I have made a conscious decision to elect the right subjects to qualify for the job. I have taken advanced courses in Geography and Political Science. I am not only good at demographics but also have great knowledge of the political landscape of Scotland, England and the east European region. Besides the core subjects, I have technology as one of my electives.

With this background, I submit my candidature for your kind consideration.

With best regards,

Sanchez Roberti”

Once convinced, she emailed it along with her resume to the address she had noted from the kiosk. It was a Friday when she wrote to them, and it wasn’t until the following Friday that she received a response.

She decided to spend that weekend with Aunt Nina. It was a lazy afternoon, and she came directly to her aunt’s place from college. Like always, she headed straight upstairs, where the desktop computer was stationed.

She logged in to her email account in anticipation of a response from TransPacific, and there it was! Her heart was pulsating so hard that for a moment she feared it would explode. With sheer nervousness, she clicked on the mail and waited impatiently for it to open. She tried hard to control her excitement as she read.

“Dear Ms. Roberti,

We have received your candidature for the job, and we take great pleasure in inviting you to the next round of evaluation. You are required to come in person for an interview.

Please bring an ID, all certificates and any recommendations that you consider relevant.

We look forward to meeting you.

Regards,

June Wield

Recruitment Coordinator”

She was on top of the world. She couldn’t believe what she had read. No matter how many times she read it, it still didn’t sink in. She could have stayed in that room reading it for the next few hours, but Aunt Nina called her for supper. Before scurrying away, she wrote a thank-you note to the recruitment officer, acknowledged receipt of the mail and confirmed that she would come for the interview. She was beaming with elation for the first time in a long while, and Aunt Nina didn’t shy away from complimenting her.

She had planned ahead in anticipation of the interview call. She had asked her literature and Geography professors for recommendation letters. She was a straight ‘A’ student. Much to the envy of her classmates, her literature professor was accused of favouring her over others. Both the professors were pleased to learn about her application, and they gladly provided their recommendation notes. She already had a distinction certificate in computers, and fortunately that year she had also won the inter-college debate competition.

She spent the next two days overthinking everything that could go wrong in the interview and what mitigation strategies she would deploy.

After a long, excruciating wait, it was finally Monday. She was at the office thirty minutes early. She put on the best attire possible, a pair of dress trousers she had got for her concert the previous year and a white shirt that she had bought with her savings over the weekend. About fifteen minutes after her arrival, she saw two more candidates join her. There were three of them – clearly, she was the youngest, at least by the look of it. She was a first-year college student, and usually people came for intern jobs in their final year. In fact, that was her biggest apprehension. She was scared to be rejected for being too young for the job.

At about 8:50 AM, the receptionist called her name and she was led into a conference room. The room appeared inviting. It was a well-lit space, large enough to fit four people. There were two people in the room waiting for her. She greeted her audience and they reciprocated with some grim smiles. The room that had appeared inviting suddenly turned into a suffocating zone. She tried her best to retain her composure as she settled on a chair across from them.

The first person introduced himself as David Buchanan, customer relationship manager. He was sitting right across from her, probably in his late thirties, and wearing a plaid coat with a checked shirt. Right next to him was a young lady named Amanda Pearl, looking graceful in her black suit and white blouse.

She handed them copies of her resume and recommendation letters. As they were flipping through the pages, David launched his first question.

“Tell me more about yourself.”

She told him about the village she belonged to, her family, academic achievements, her interest in gymnastics, coming to Ayr for higher studies, awards she had won and her hobbies. She mentioned everything except a few things, which she concealed for reasons best known to her.

David continued with a few more questions about gymnastics and the awards she had won. He was particularly interested in her recent debate win. Sanchez provided him with all the necessary details. After the first few questions, she was at ease, much to her relief. All her fears had disappeared.

David continued, “What do you know about TransPacific?”

That was an easy question for her – she had done exhaustive research on TransPacific. She was sure that she knew more about the company than her immediate audience. She provided them with a nice summary, right from how it was founded, its values and credo, the acquisitions it had made over time, recent news, geographical spread, financial performance, key leaders’ insights and its future growth strategy. Though they both held an impassive posture, she could clearly see how impressed they were.

Amanda, the HR person, broke her silence. Her question was more about how choosing this job would help her career progression. A prelude to this was in her initial email to them, and she elaborated on it to their satisfaction.

David took the reins back from Amanda and put forward a difficult question. “Let us assume you are on a customer call and the person is in extreme frustration about an undelivered package. You cannot trace the package either, and you need to reach out to the shipping team for help. Meanwhile, the customer is furious and making all kinds of threats. How will you handle the situation?”

It was a tough question to answer, particularly when she hardly had any time to weigh her options. But that was the prime intent behind the question, as he wanted her to respond impromptu.

She took a pause, recalibrated her thoughts and responded.

“If the situation is that bad and I can’t resolve the problem, I will have to approach it a little differently. I will first empathise with his situation. I will let him know that I am unable to see his order in the system and need to put him on hold briefly to talk to the shipping department. When a person is frustrated, the last thing you want to do is put the person on hold. As I may not be sure how long it will take, I will come back on the line at regular intervals to update him. No matter how many hops it takes, I will not transfer the call. I will also get a number I can call him back on in case the line gets dropped. In situations like this, giving reassurance is key. If I am unable to resolve his problem, I will have my supervisor available to speak to him.”

She was in complete control of the conversation, and she knew that after that response she had secured the job. After some casual probing, they finally came to the last question. “Any questions for us?”

While she was tempted to inquire how she fared, she controlled her anxiousness. She gave a tight-lipped smile and asked about the next steps.

She got a general answer that someone from human resources would get in touch with her should they decide to go forward. With that, she thanked them and stepped out of the room.

She looked at her watch and was amazed to find that she had spent a good forty-five minutes with them. She was delighted and headed straight to college to continue with her schedule.

She didn’t hear anything from them until the following Monday. There was a mail congratulating her on being chosen as an intern. There was some online paperwork that she had to get done, a drug test and a contract that she was required to sign.

She had no clue how to react to the news. At first she was numb, and then a sudden surge of emotions overtook her. She cried, she laughed, and she screamed. It was a huge milestone, and she dearly hoped all the answers she had been looking for all these years were hidden somewhere in TransPacific. She was raring to unearth them.

**Chapter 14**

***16th April 2012, Ayr, Scotland***

Her job as a summer intern was starting in two weeks, and her eagerness to begin was testing her patience. After a long wait, the day finally arrived. She was assigned to the customer service department, reporting to David Buchanan, the person who had interviewed her.

He was one of the nicest people she met at TransPacific. He was kind, gave her a lot of space and helped her learn the business. She developed an instant liking for him. The first few days were tough. She was overwhelmed by the size and scale of the work. But David’s support in those initial days helped her immensely to overcome her apprehensions.

She was on the customer service operator floor.  She had no job assigned. The only instruction she received was to help the call agents whenever they needed her. She spent the first two weeks observing the work, learning how they operated and the processes they followed. After her induction, she was at the disposal of the team leads on the ground. Most of the time she was running between agents to pass information or generating some arbitrary reports. While working for a large corporation was exciting, she made sure not to drift away from her goal of finding John Patterson.

It took two weeks before she was assigned a desktop computer, something she had been eagerly waiting for. That day she decided to stay back in the office to begin her research.

At 6:00 PM, the day shift left. The night shift people were slowly coming in, and the floor was sparsely filled. She looked around and found no one paying any attention to her. The time looked right for her to initiate her search. With extreme caution, she opened the global address list on her computer and searched for John Patterson. As the receptionist had mentioned, she found four people with the same name. She looked through the details of each one of them to see if she could draw any meaningful insights.

The first John was a vice president of international operations. She ruled him out, as he didn’t seem to be the person who would carry freight around. The second John had a field job. He was from the far north-east of Scotland, a place called Fraserburgh. Though his job profile was akin to what she was looking for, the distance between her village and Fraserburgh wasn’t convincing enough. It was too far for anyone to make deliveries on a regular basis. She put this John aside as her fallback and moved on to the remaining two names. The third John was in South Africa. Putting that one aside, she opened the last John’s details.

And as fate would have it, this John was a perfect match. He was from Ayr, and sure enough, he was a freight delivery person.  She hurriedly noted his number and office stop. Her feet were trembling with fear, and she was perspiring. All she wanted was to leave the office as fast as she could before anyone noticed her fidgety state.

**Chapter 15**

***30th April 2012, Ayr, Scotland***

Though not confirmed, she had a strong intuition of being very close to the truth she was seeking. The first months in Ayr were testing times; often she was dejected and clueless.  But with the job she had found a purpose, a resurrection from her shattered life, and she was enthralled by the possibilities that had surged back with urgency.

On her way back home, as she sat alone on the bus, a thought crossed her mind, leaving her scared – if a neophyte like her could find John Patterson, why had the cops failed? Or did they consciously stay away for reasons unknown? Something was not right, and it disturbed her. There was more to this episode, a conspiracy, a subterfuge she wasn’t aware of. The more her mind indulged in the quandary, the more uncomfortable and restless she became. She forced her mind away from the shackles of the past incidents and brought her focus back to John Patterson. After a lot of deliberation, she concocted a plan and now she was raring to execute it.

Her next day went by quickly, mostly in running tasks for the team. She was now well versed in the customer service process. She also gave a couple of significant data curating ideas. David was extremely happy with her contributions and gave her the go-ahead to pilot one of the concepts she had proposed. Incidentally, there was an organisation-wide innovation quest going on, which was run by the corporate office. David encouraged her to put forward her idea for the contest.

Soon it was evening. She had planned beforehand to stay late. She patiently waited for the office people to leave. Once convinced that no one was around, she picked up one of the agents’ phones and called John’s number. After a few rings, she heard a deep voice on the other end.

Sanchez cleared her throat. “Am I speaking to John Patterson?”

“Yes, you are.”

“I am calling from Mr. Cussak’s office, and there is an important packet he wants me to deliver to you. You can either come to the office or I can deliver it to your home. Sorry for the inconvenience, but the package needs immediate attention.”

Mr. Cussak was John’s reporting manager’s superior, and her hope was that he wouldn’t risk saying no. Also, as Cussak wasn’t John’s immediate reporting manager, her belief was that John wouldn’t call him to confirm either.

“Do you know what I need to do with the packet?” came a puzzled response from John.

The conversation was progressing as she anticipated.

“I wouldn’t know. But there is a sealed letter that has all the instructions. Would you prefer to come to the office or do you want me to deliver it to you?”

He took a long pause, and with clear reluctance in his voice said, “It would be great if you could deliver it. The address is 254 Brendstad Street, Ayr.”

She thanked him and promised to deliver it within the next hour. She couldn’t believe she had John’s address in her possession. She had a strong intuition that this John was the one she was looking for.  Though she knew it was a huge risk, it hardly deterred her from going ahead with her plan.

She ditched her usual bus ride and took a cab instead. Throughout the journey, she kept rehearsing the script she planned to narrate to him. She was agitated and tried her best to keep her anxieties under control. The traffic was denser than usual, and it took her about forty-five minutes to reach John’s residence, longer than she had expected. The taxi driver dropped her on the street right across from his home. It was an old block of row houses. With her mind clouded with numerous apprehensions, she gently tapped on the door. After a couple of knocks, a dishevelled-looking man appeared at the door. He was in his late fifties, appearing older than his age because of his worn-out look. He was wearing a pair of tattered jeans and a T-shirt, which had the official emblem of the Bohemian Football club.

She quickly introduced herself as the person from Mr. Cussak’s office. He looked at her empty hands, and with a confused look responded, “You said you have a packet for me.”

She acknowledged his observation and immediately launched into what she had practised relentlessly on her way to his home.

“Would you be kind enough to allow me inside your house? I want a few minutes of your time and I will explain everything to you,” Sanchez blurted in a most awkward way, not a shade close to what she had rehearsed.

He appeared irresolute and his countenance displayed shades of bewilderment. “What is it about?”

He sounded displeased, and Sanchez was losing the plot at a rapid pace. She continued, “I promise it won’t take much time. I need a few minutes to explain.”

The old man took a pause and it appeared to be the longest minute of the evening for Sanchez. With dwindling hope and pleading eyes, she kept looking at him. After pondering for a while, and with a reluctant look, he gave in to her request and instructed her to wait outside. He was back in a minute with his shoes on. “Let us take a walk instead.”

The by-lanes in the neighborhood were quiet, with a few sporadic evening commuters gracing the streets. As they went past his home, he snapped, “Tell me what is on your mind. Do you even work for TransPacific?”

Without raising any further alarm, she clarified that she did work for TransPacific, but the entire packet delivery episode was a farce. She told him that she had to meet him, and she couldn’t find any other way. Though she apologised with profound genuineness, it failed to make any change in his agitated expression. He looked wild and remarked, “You better have a good explanation for your misdeed, or else you are heading for big trouble.”

It was time for her to execute her script. She pulled herself out of her confusion and hesitantly continued, “Mr. Patterson, let me come straight to the point. Has your business taken you to Girbin in the past? Do you know anyone from there?”

He stopped abruptly and stared at her with wide-open eyes. His face turned pale. A thousand emotions were crossing his face, and from being confused he soon became scared. Observing his flustered demeanour, she was certain she had found the John she was looking for. Without giving him much time to react, she proceeded, “Mr. Patterson, I have no intention to upset you or harm you. All I want is answers to some questions. Questions that I have been seeking answers to for the past two years…”

Her voice trailed, reflecting a shade of brittleness. “I need your help. Please say something.” By now she couldn’t contain her emotions anymore and looked breathless and shaky.

He stood there dumbstruck for a while. And when he realised what had struck him, he blurted, “I don’t know what you are talking about. I need to go now,” and hurried back towards his home.

Seeing him flee, she dashed to narrow the distance between them. She grabbed his hand from behind, making him slow down. “Mr. Patterson, trust me, what you tell me will remain with me. Please… you are my only hope.”

He pulled his hand away and, without saying anything, surged ahead, leaving her disheartened behind. She stood there for a moment or two before leaping after him. He was around the corner, and she dashed with all her might to get in front of him. He snapped and warned her to stay away from him. He also threatened her with consequences if she came after him. She had no option but to let him go. One thing was certain. He knew something, and he was clearly avoiding any confrontation. For the next several weeks she tried talking to him, but without success. She even stalked him, confronted him, tried hard to strike up a conversation, but nothing seemed to work. All her plans were losing steam and it appeared as if she was at a dead end, with nowhere to go.

**Chapter 16**

***17th June 2012, Ayr, Scotland***

Two months had gone by since her last confrontation with John, and she had barely made any progress. Her overindulgent mind was desperate to find some headway but was miserably failing. On a Sunday afternoon, lost in her chaotic quandary, she pulled her father’s notebook from the closet.  While she was languidly flipping through the pages, something caught her attention. She noticed John’s name scribbled in many places. It was marked against addresses, in footnotes and as part of some to-do list items. Right at that moment, she had an epiphany. She was almost certain that this time John would give in to her demand.

She took her chances and headed straight to his house. It was a grey afternoon with few people on the streets. She reached the door without anyone noticing her presence. The house appeared deserted with no sign of any living soul around. She tried peeking through the glass door, but the curtains were shielding her vision. With some hesitation, she gently knocked on the door and patiently waited for a response. The gentle knocks turned hard, and after repeated attempts John responded. She must have woken him from his afternoon sleep, as his long hair was ruffled and scattered all over his wrinkled face. His somnolence vanished as soon as he saw her. He almost closed the door on her, but she used all her might to hold it open and exclaimed, “If you don’t listen to me, soon you will need to answer to the police.”

That worked. All his resistance vanished, and with dropped shoulders, he said, “Why don’t you leave me alone? You don’t know what you are looking for. It will ruin us.”

She pushed herself into his house as if she had an unstated decree to be in his living room. This time the old man hardly resisted and slouched on the sofa. He appeared defeated, and by his looks he had certainly submitted himself to the situation.

Sanchez swiftly followed him and sat on the chair right across from him. Without wasting any time, she continued, “Mr. Patterson, as I said before, I have no intention to harm you. I want to know what happened, and trust me, once I have the truth you are out of it. No one will know anything.”

He was eager to escape. In a low tone, he said, “You got to trust me. I hardly know anything to tell you.”

She was hesitant to interrupt his flow of thoughts and kept her anxiety under control. He was gazing at the ceiling as if trying to recollect the old times, and continued, “It was something to do with that package...” His voice trailed, and he became silent.

Her mouth was dry. She couldn’t hold her nerves any longer and blurted, “What package? Who sent the package?”

“I have no idea. All I can tell you is that the package instruction came from the head office – it came from Evan Slater, who heads sales operations for Scotland and England. Please keep me out of it. You need to believe me. I don’t know anything beyond this.” His eyes were pleading. There was sincerity in his request, and he clearly wanted to escape from her trap.

She had another name to chase now. Before letting him go, she had one final question for him – why was he so scared about the situation? But he didn’t have any answer for her. He kept gazing at the floor, as he couldn’t face her anymore.

She thanked him and assured him that whatever he had said would stay with her. It appeared as if he had got himself out of a guilt he had been carrying for a while, and he regained a sense of calmness.

The hunt was becoming complicated for her. Though she had a new lead, there were more questions than answers. There was a distinct sense of accomplishment, an excitement that engulfed her, yet there was an uncertainty that snatched all her exhilaration away.

**Chapter 17**

***18th June 2012, Ayr, Scotland***

It wasn’t an arduous job to trace Evan Slater. A quick search on the global address list was all she had to do to locate him.  He was the executive chairman of sales operations, and in his profile picture, he appeared to be in his early fifties, depending on when the photo was taken. She did some further research and learned he was one of the three people who founded TransPacific. He was a self-made person, ranked in the top 300 on the Forbes list of billionaires. To her astonishment, besides this fact, not much was written about him in the public domain. He had not spoken at any conferences or been interviewed by any media. Compared to the other founders of TransPacific, he was the least mentioned. She continued her research for several days but failed to locate anything meaningful. Tired of her failures, she concluded that he was either an introvert or extremely particular about his privacy. She thought of visiting him, but access to the executive floor was restricted.  Nothing was working, and she was struggling to find a wedge to break away from the snare she found herself trapped in.

Months went by without any progress. Though she was left with no options, she resisted admitting defeat. She was bereft of recourse but not hope. She knew her day would come. With no lead in hand, her job at TransPacific kept her going. David was pleased with the impact she had created in a short period of her association with TransPacific. The improvement idea she gave to the team on process quality got shortlisted as one of the top three innovative ideas across the organisation, and she was invited to present it to an evaluation committee.

She was delighted to learn that the chief guest was none other than Evan Slater. The forces of nature were favouring her. She knew her intent was noble and considered the event a medium to reach Evan Slater. She prepared well for the event – she revised her idea, tested it with the team, created a running report from the pilot she had executed before and quantified the outcomes. She was ready for the day.

On the day of the presentation, all the participants were called up to the executive floor. There were three finalists including her, and they were required to present their idea to a panel of judges. The executive conference room was one of the swankiest places she had ever seen. The room was octagonal in shape, spacious enough to fit about thirty people.  The big glass-top table in the centre was also octagonal, like a miniature version of the room. There were ergonomically designed Aeron chairs sparsely arranged around the table, and each seat had a leather-bound notepad and a Waterman ballpoint pen placed with impeccable precision. Across the table, there was an eighty-inch television set with state-of-the-art meeting room gadgets. Two of the walls were covered with mahogany bookshelves, with classic hard-bound books arranged in an immaculate manner.  The room was on the edge of the 15th floor and the glass on the side wall gave a picturesque view of the port city. Despite the room being intimidating, to her surprise she was at ease, ready to embrace the challenge. All the participants were in the room about thirty minutes early. The conference room assistant helped the participants set up their presentations, and they were ready for the showdown.

Five minutes before the event began, she saw the executives walking in. The panel of judges comprised five people, representing the five divisions of TransPacific. They took the seats close to the display. One of the judges was Evan Slater. His appearance was exactly like his profile picture. He was wearing a dark suit, looking sharp, and his face was beaming with a radiant smile. Besides the judges, there were the business managers of the three divisions whose ideas were selected for the competition, and David was one amongst them. There were also people whom she couldn’t identify, but by the looks, they appeared to be from the executive office. Unlike what she had assumed, the executives were extremely friendly. They put the participants at ease and congratulated them on making it to the finals. After the initial introductions, it was show time. Each finalist got about fifteen minutes to present their idea.

Sanchez was the last one to go. She was confident for a few reasons. First, her idea had a direct impact on savings. Her pilot proved that she could save the company upwards of five million pounds over five years, the highest amongst her competition. Second, she had a working prototype while the others had a concept on paper. And she was good at public presentation.

The presentations were done on time, and the participants were requested to wait in an adjacent room. After about twenty minutes, they were called back in, and she was announced the winner.

Besides getting some bonus money and a certificate of acknowledgment, she got what she least expected, a chance to work with Evan Slater directly. She couldn’t believe it, and her emotions got the best of her. Much against her wish, a trickle of tears appeared at the corners of her eyes. She wasn’t sure whether the sniffling was out of joy or because she had managed to break away from her chaotic quandary.

Evan congratulated her and said he was looking forward to seeing her in his office. After Evan had gone, a man walked up to her and introduced himself as Bradley Fawcett. He stated that he was from Evan’s executive office and instructed her to come and meet him in his office the next day. He gave her his card and swiftly walked out of the room.

**Chapter 18**

***15th October 2012, Ayr, Scotland***

Bradley was the programme manager responsible for executing multiple mission-critical programmes for Evan’s office. He became Sanchez’s new reporting manager. Her job was to ensure that her idea got implemented and the benefits delivered. One thing she indisputably understood was that TransPacific recognised talent, as providing an intern with such a big responsibility was a rare sight in any corporate setup.

Life at TransPacific became extremely busy for her. Over the period of her stay, she found Bradley to be astute and a manager with amazing leadership skills. She learned a lot about managing project timelines, delivering quality work and getting milestones accomplished. The first few months went by swiftly. Despite being close to Evan’s office, she made no progress on her personal agenda. Partly it was because Bradley was omnipresent. He ensured she always had company. He was playing the role of a responsible leader helping his team member to succeed. Besides, he was the first one to come and the last one to leave the office. His protective demeanour wasn’t helping her investigations.

She realised the only way she could be on her own was by coming to the office earlier than Bradley.  She decided to be in the office by 6:00 AM, well before Bradley’s arrival. While alone in the office, she looked through files, searched office drawers and peeked into Evan’s workplace, but she couldn’t find anything meaningful.

It was two months into her new role when one day she noticed some strange behaviour in the office. A high-profile delegate was visiting Evan, and the entire floor got cordoned off. All employees were confined to a small zone with strict instructions not to trespass. She saw the incident repeat a few times in the next few months. No one knew the reason behind such an outlandish demand. And to her disbelief, people followed the routine and never questioned it.

Evan’s office assistant, a young lady in her mid-twenties, became a good friend of Sanchez. One day over the lunch table, Sanchez casually enquired about the fuss created during such visits. While her friend reserved her comments, the only part she revealed was a name, Benedikt, and how the office became a secured zone whenever he was around.

It must have been a week into her early morning ventures when Bradley abruptly appeared right as she entered the office building. With a stern voice, he commanded her to see him in his office.

She entered his cabin. She was petrified, and no matter how much she tried, she couldn’t shrug away her startled mien. He didn’t greet her, and with a demanding tone, he probed why she was snooping around. The earth caved in on her, and her mind went numb. She tried her best to regain her composure and responded softly that she had no clue what he was talking about.

His eyes were searching her, making her lose any remaining nerve. He declared he was aware of all her proceedings and had the videos to prove it. He further announced that so far only he knew about it, but if she refused to tell the truth, he would be forced to involve the security team.

She was caught red-handed. Her mind was whirling hard to spin a story, and out of nervousness she blurted the wrong words, “It is out of sheer inquisitiveness. I was eager to find out why we are put in isolation when Benedikt arrives.”

His next question was how she knew about Benedikt.  She didn’t want to put Evan’s office assistant in any trouble and gave a vague answer, stating she herself couldn’t recall how she knew his name.

Bradley instructed her to take a seat and offered her some water, which she graciously accepted. Her guard was down, and she was starkly exposed. His gazing eyes were disturbing her the most. After a couple of long minutes he stated, “Look, I know you are not telling me the truth, but honestly, I don’t care. I have no interest in why you are sniffing around. However, I am interested in you.”

He took a long pause. She was puzzled by the sudden change in his stance. She kept quiet and waited patiently for him to continue. “I can help you with what you are looking for.  Let us head out for a cup of coffee. Shall we?”

She didn’t react and meekly followed him. Right outside the office, there was an Italian coffee shop. They both settled down with a cup of black coffee. Without mincing any words, he continued, “Evan is running a large scam. I am not sure what they do, but I am certain they are delivering goods across a route that starts from Europe and goes all the way to Russia. The plan of the route is decided every three months, and Benedikt is its custodian. Once the route is defined, they all meet to discuss things. There is no one fixed place for the meeting. This time it was in Ayr. A lot of senior executives of TransPacific, high-ranking government officials and politicians are part of this. The reason I am telling you all this is that I want you to join me and help me collect evidence against them. You are intelligent, beautiful and you are different. We can form a good team.” He took a pause and continued, “Remember, this discussion remains between us. If you try to act smart and tell anyone about it, I will expose you. No one will believe your story once I show the videos.”

She had no option but to say yes. However, she was curious to know more about him. Though hesitant, she still asked, “Who are you? Why are you after Evan?”

He brusquely responded, “I will never bother to know about you, and you shouldn’t bother about me.”

She agreed, as that way she could keep her secrets. Confused, she innocently enquired what he wanted from her.

“For now, I want you to join a gym, a kickboxing class, professional karate and rock climbing. You are already enrolled – make sure you learn the skills with utmost sincerity. Trust me, you will need them in your quest to find out more about Evan.” There was a shade of evilness in his smirk that made her uncomfortable.

Though surprised, she timidly accepted what he offered. For the next year, all she did was train her body. She was frustrated. At times she desired to escape, but shrugged away the thought, knowing what Bradley was capable of doing. After about a year, they had their first real conversation, the discussion about getting details of the map from Benedikt.

She left her job at TransPacific and dissolved into oblivion. Surprisingly, she never saw Bradley again, nor did he approach her. He was like a mysterious character from some gothic setting. She was thankful to him not only for the lead but also for the training, which had come in so handy in getting her hands on the map. She was unsure where the map would lead her, but she had a strong hunch that it would be the medium to reveal the mysteries she had been hunting for the past few years.

**PART III**

**Chapter 19**

***4:00 AM 16th April 2014, Zurich, Switzerland***

The train halted abruptly, pulling Maxwell out of his slumber. For the first few seconds, he was in a state of oblivion, trying hard to figure out his whereabouts. The last few days had been nothing but living life on the edge. And as reality sank in, his first reflex was to ensure Sanchez was still on the train. He craned his neck sideways to peep into the compartment but couldn’t find her anywhere.

He transformed from his lethargic self to a sprinter with a purpose. He dashed to the train compartment to reconfirm her absence, and once convinced, he leaped out of the train with his eyes searching for her in all directions. It was the wee hours, and to his advantage, there were few people on the platform. He scanned each one of them with prying eyes, and then he saw her not too far away from him, scurrying towards the exit. He scooted back onto the train, grabbed his bag and went after her. He moved with watchful steps, taking care not to make the chase look too obvious. From a distance, he saw her disappearing behind the doors of a BMW parked right by the kerb of the inner driveway of the station exit.

This wasn’t part of his plan. Sanchez had taken him by complete surprise. He had a well-laid-out scheme to trail her upon her arrival in Basel. And here he was, in the middle of Zurich without any support system, dishevelled and frustrated. He saw a taxi stand next to the exit, and he dashed towards it with all his might. He got into the first one, flashed the driver his identity card and commanded him to follow the BMW.

The taxi driver was startled. Though reluctant, he followed the instructions, unsure what had struck him. Maxwell’s mind was racing. He tried to comprehend Sanchez’s sudden change in plan but failed to conclude anything meaningful. The streets were busy because of the coming spring festival, and he didn’t mind it at all, for it helped him to camouflage. After about fifteen minutes of following her, he observed the car making some random turns as if she was aware of being followed. He instructed the taxi driver to slow down and directed him to stay about three to four cars behind the BMW.

His doubt was confirmed when he observed the BMW continue taking some erratic paths. He had no option but to follow, for losing sight of her was a risk he couldn’t afford. As he was contemplating his next move, he saw the car taking a sharp turn into a side lane. It took him a couple of minutes to make that turn, and when he finally got to the cross street, the BMW was nowhere to be seen. He forced the taxi driver to accelerate. There was a big apartment complex on the left. He peeked at the entrance as he went past it and got a glimpse of the car.  He asked the driver to take the next right and stop by the kerb. Using a rideshare app on his phone, he called another taxi. The cab was about five minutes away, and he hurried to look for a temporary shelter. He had a strong intuition that someone would come looking for him. He saw a coffee shop at the street intersection. He bolted in, ordered a cappuccino and took the corner seat by the door. From where he was sitting, he had a clear sight of all four corners of the street.  The cab arrived on time. He instructed the driver to turn on the metre and wait for him.

His guess was right. In about five minutes he saw a young man in his early twenties scanning the streets. The man spent a few minutes observing the street with cautious eyes. Once satisfied with his investigation, he turned back towards the apartment building. The moment his back faced the street, Maxwell leaped out of the coffee shop and waited at the kerb to see where he was heading. Sure enough, he was going to the opposite intersection to continue his search. Maxwell rushed to the parked taxi and instructed the driver to keep waiting a few more minutes. The taxi driver appeared upset, and before he could complain, Maxwell announced his identity. He refrained from taking any chances this time and instructed the driver to take the passenger seat. The driver had mixed emotions, but Maxwell had bigger problems to solve than worrying about his companion.

Ten minutes into the wait, he saw the BMW rolling out of the apartment complex. It made a left on the street and headed north. This time Maxwell was extra prudent. He kept the taxi at a distance and traced the path with added stealth and alertness. He was cognizant that the extended distance between the two cars might result in losing sight of Sanchez. With no better alternative, he made the conscious decision to take the calculated risk. After a few turns, he saw the BMW heading towards the A3 motorway. He was relieved, as trailing a car on a highway was much easier than in the lattice of Zurich streets. After about two hours of driving on the A3, he saw the BMW taking the highway exit. To his advantage, a few more cars were exiting the highway. It helped him blend his car with the other vehicles into a perfect disguise. The BMW drove for another ten minutes before it turned into a small lane.

Maxwell avoided the turn to ensure he didn’t give himself any undue exposure, especially when the chase was almost coming to an end. As he passed the lane where Sanchez’s car had turned, he cast a fleeting glance to see which way it travelled. He couldn’t see the BMW but managed to see a blue sign stating in German “Sackgasse” – an evil smirk crossed his lips as he knew the street led to a dead end. He was certain they would be in one of the houses on that lane.

At the next intersection, he got out of the taxi. The taxi fled with a screeching noise, as the driver tried to get away from the ordeal as fast as he could. He gave himself a few minutes before starting to walk towards the lane where the BMW had turned not too long ago. He paced his walk, which was a little shy of a dash. His ferreting eyes were looking for the car. About 600 feet into the lane, he saw another lane cutting to the left. He had a strong intuition that he would find the car in that lane and followed his instincts. As he walked through the narrow pathway, he saw a row of two-storied apartments. Maxwell was scrutinising the parked cars, looking for the BMW. After passing the first two blocks, he saw the car neatly parked in the front row facing the street. He saw only one apartment in the building with the lights on. Despite the sheer darkness, it would have been hard for anyone to miss the smile that graced his face.

He knew he had only a few hours to execute his plan, for he was certain they would soon leave the place. He had some chores to do before they left the apartment. Now that all the commotion was behind him, he was famished and had a sudden urge for black coffee. He retraced his path back to the main street. There was a gas station right across the street. The convenience store by the gas station was the only possible place to get anything in the early morning hours. He ordered a coffee and picked up a cold sandwich from the refrigerator. There was a small sitting area by the café, and he settled down with his early morning nibbles. After devouring his sandwich, he pulled out his phone and called someone, and was in a discussion for about ten minutes. He got a call after a thirty-minute wait. He stepped out of the café and walked towards an Audi R8, parked outside the store. A man in his mid-thirties stepped out and handed him a packet. He shook his hand and slipped into the R8 while the man disappeared into the coldness of the morning mist.

Maxwell parked the Audi at the entrance of the lane where Sanchez was sheltered. He trod the path with extra vigilance.  He was by the block where Sanchez’s dwelling was located. And his inquisitive eyes searched for the apartment lights. Sure enough, the lights were off, and as he had conjectured, they were reposing.

It was the early hours of a lazy Wednesday. Besides, the coldness had put all souls in their shelters. Taking the morning mist as his shield, he sprinted into action. He had a transmitter, which he installed under the boot of the BMW, a little away from the exhaust. He turned the transmitter on and checked the signal on his GPS. The GPS came to life and displayed the coordinates of the BMW on its small screen. It had been a long chase, and now that he had taken care of what was important, he could look forward to getting some rest. He walked back to his Audi, drove it to the next street, parked it in an empty spot, reclined the seat and dozed off. This time he trusted the receiver’s beep to keep a check on his sleep. He had neither the verve nor any energy left to be paranoid and stay awake.

**Chapter 20**

***4:00 AM 17th April 2014, Helsingør, Denmark***

The loud chime of the phone ruffled the quietness of the night. This was the first time Benedikt had slept with some peace since Sanchez disappeared.  But the phone refused to give up. Benedikt jolted to consciousness and vaguely looked around for the phone. He found it between the sofa cushions he was resting on. He fumbled, and after a few attempts succeeded in hitting the answer button and responded with a gravelly voice.

“Chief, it is me. I have some important information to give you.” There was a spark of excitement in Damian’s voice.

“Go on.” Benedikt’s senses were alert, and he reached to switch on the lights.

“The CCTV footage at the Zurich train station clearly captured Sanchez getting off the train early in the morning. Trevor was on that train too. The way he approached the exit makes me believe that he was following her.”

Benedikt looked at his watch. It was 4:00 AM. If he left now, he could be in Zurich by 7:00. “I will be there by 7:00. Is there anything else you found about them?”

“I also checked the security cameras of the main entrance. She left in a BMW. Though the image wasn’t clear, I still managed to get the plate number. Trevor left in a cab. The cab waiting area was at the far left, away from the camera. I tried all possible ways but couldn’t trace the plate number of the taxi.”

Benedikt was wide-awake by now and eager to know more.  “Whom does the BMW belong to? Did you find out about the taxi driver?”

Damian continued, “We may have to wait until morning to get the BMW details. Russ has not traced the transport registration person yet. Also, I couldn’t locate the taxi driver. The taxi area by Zurich station is open to all drivers. I tried checking with the drivers at the stand, but unfortunately, no one noticed him getting into a cab. I am on it and I am hopeful of getting a lead soon.”

Benedikt started getting dressed and made his final point, “I am leaving now and will see you in a couple of hours.”

He ended the call, picked up his travel bag and tapped a number on his phone. “Get the jet ready. I will be there in forty-five minutes. Also, get the route and clearance for Zurich.”

As Benedikt walked out of the lobby, a cold wave of wind sent shivers through his spine, taking him by surprise, and his tiredness faded away in a flash. His mind was in constant battle with his predicaments. This was his chance to salvage the situation, to confront Sanchez and to conquer some unstated desires.

**Chapter 21**

***7:00 AM 17th April 2014, Zurich, Switzerland***

It was a little shy of 7:00 AM when Benedikt landed in Zurich. The private transfer at Zurich allowed Damian to take his car straight to the tarmac where Benedikt’s jet was cooling down. In a few minutes, the retractable air stairs flung open from the clamshell-shaped plane door and Benedikt started descending.

No matter what the situation or occasion was, Benedikt never got his sense of dressing wrong. He was wearing a dark blue woolen double-breasted Armani suit. Underneath the suit was a neatly fitted waistcoat, and gracing his shirt was a single-knot sky blue tie. An unbuttoned London-Fog trench coat was covering his stretched arms and torso. His dark brown shoulder-length hair was brushed back, and his eyes were covered with dark blue shades. The wind, coupled with the gust of air from the jet wings, was making his trench coat make a surging clamour noise, aptly complementing his demeanour.

As soon as he was on the final step of the air stairs, Damian lunged forward and opened the rear door of the car. Benedikt ignored the invite and opened the passenger side door instead. A helper brought Benedikt’s luggage and put it in the open boot. Damian looped around and took the driver’s seat, and within minutes they were out of the airport on to the service lane.

“What is the update?”

Damian, without waiting any further, started speaking, “We were able to get the car’s details. It is a Hertz rental car. Someone rented the car under the name Stephen Roberti.”

“Isn’t Sanchez’s last name also Roberti?” Benedikt’s voice reflected inquisitiveness.

“Yes. There must be some relationship between them. Also, the car is rented until tomorrow. And...”

Benedikt interrupted Damian with his next question, “Where did they rent the car from?”

“They rented it from the Zurich airport, but it is interesting to note that they requested to return the car at St. Gallen-Altenrhein airport. I have booked you in the Ritz close to the airport. After dropping you, I will go to the rental office and join Russ, who is on his way to the airport.”

Benedikt’s face turned red, and from his look anyone could guess he was on the verge of losing his cool. He tried his best to control his nerves. “I have had my share of sleep. Let us head straight to the airport.” He looked at his watch. It was 7:15 AM. “How long will it take to the airport?”

“Once we are on the A1 motorway, it will be less than an hour. We should be there by 8:30 AM.”

Benedikt knew he was running out of time. He had no option but to hope he could get hold of Sanchez. “Drive as fast as you can,” he told Damian. He had a few more questions for him, but he decided to wait. His prime motive was to reach the airport at the earliest possible time. He pulled out his phone, called someone and instructed the person on the other side to find out about Sanchez’s flight details.

The A1 motorway was lean in traffic, and Damian was doing a fine job of maneuvering at the highest speed one could possibly drive without getting into trouble. Benedikt pondered hard to find some way to get hold of Sanchez. His superiors had given him only the next three days to bring it to a closure. Regardless, he had to bring this untoward nuisance to an end, as starting Monday he had to be in Tallinn to meet the Russian delegates. Wednesday was when project “Sub Rosa” would commence, which couldn’t be compromised at any cost.

He was jolted out of his brooding as the seat belt pulled him abruptly when Damian applied the brakes. The cars were congregating, and the speed dropped from 160 km/h to 30 km/h. Benedikt lowered the window and stretched his neck out to see what had caused the holdup, but couldn’t find anything. He was about ten minutes into the stall now, and his annoyance was extreme. He wasn’t a happy man, and he was giving Damian the jitters although none of it was his fault. After dragging along for about fifteen minutes, he witnessed the reason. The left lane was closed, causing the cars to divert on to the remaining two operating lanes. The exasperation on Benedikt’s face was blatantly visible. He couldn’t hold himself back and blurted out, “The damn municipality workers – not sure what they are constructing on this cold morning. Wasted fifteen minutes for nothing.”

He looked at his watch. It was 8:35 AM. Every minute was diminishing his hope exponentially, and the delay wasn’t helping him keep his composure. To add to the misery, Russ was badly stuck in some accident traffic and was even further behind.

They arrived at the rental car area without any further delays. Damian took the car straight to the rental office, parked it, and both rushed inside. There was a person shouting from the back for parking the car at the main driving area, but they cared little to lend their ears to him. Damian enquired about the BMW in haste. And to add insult to injury, the car had been returned a while back.

Benedikt pulled out his phone and called someone. “Did you find anything?”

“Not too long back, two tickets to Lisbon were bought. It took me a while as they were bought only this morning,” the person on the other side responded with some hesitation.

“What time is the flight?” His aggravation was on the rise.

“Sorry, Chief. The flight has departed,” came the timid response from the other side.

Benedikt ended the call and screamed in frustration, “Damn! I was this close to nabbing her!”

He looked at Damian. “Get me to the jet. We are flying to Lisbon.”

As Benedikt settled himself in the car for the hour-long ride to the plane, his mind drifted off to a different world crowded with many passing thoughts. He murmured, “Why Lisbon? What is her motive?” Benedikt was certain that the whole Sanchez episode wasn’t a trivial matter. There was a game plan Sanchez had, but no matter how hard he tried to solve the mystery, he failed. He asked himself, “Operation ‘Sub Rosa’ has nothing to do with Lisbon, so why is she travelling to Lisbon?” He went into a deep rumination. “Am I giving in to a fear that might not even exist? She has nothing to do with operation ‘Sub Rosa’. Maybe she is a kleptomaniac and robbed me for fun.”   He couldn’t reason it out, and the urge to get to the bottom of it was almost unbearable.

He attempted to divert his attention by using the time to put a few questions to Damian. Without looking at him, he asked in a hushed tone, “Did you find anything about the person who was chasing Sanchez?”

“Nothing so far,” came the reply from Damian.

“Do you have a good capture of his picture?”

“I am afraid not.”

“Well then, put someone on the job. Will you?”

“Sure,” Damian responded sheepishly and called someone to work on the picture.

Benedikt dialed another number and patiently waited for the person to respond. The phone kept ringing. He tried the number three more times, but no one picked up the phone. He put the phone aside and enquired, “How much longer?”

“We should be there in the next twenty minutes.” Damian’s voice reflected clear signs of hesitation. Benedikt noted that the traffic was cruising along; ironically that wasn’t the case when he wanted it the most. He could have caught Sanchez before she boarded the plane. The phone rang and pulled him out of his brooding. He picked up the phone and snapped, “What took you so long to respond?”

“It was a mistake. I didn’t notice my phone was in silent mode. Since last night I…”

Benedikt interrupted him. “I am not interested in your story. I want you to head to Lisbon Airport now. I want you to trace and capture two people. They are travelling by TAP Portugal and will be landing in the next hour. You need to ensure you grab them and meet me at our usual place. I am on my way to Lisbon and should be there in the next few hours. Details of the passengers will be sent to you in the next fifteen minutes. Any questions?”

“Nothing for now, but I may have a few after I see the information.”

Benedikt continued, “Also, one more thing. I might send you a picture of another person. I strongly believe he is on the same flight. If you find him, get him as well.”

“Alright, Chief, point noted.”

Soon Damian pulled into the Zurich airport private transfer tarmac. The jet was waiting for Benedikt.

As Damian opened the door, Benedikt announced unceremoniously, “You are coming with me.”

Damian wasn’t surprised as he was aware of Benedikt’s unpredictable behaviour. He followed Benedikt into the jet. Soon the plane was waiting for clearance to fly.

Benedikt was all set to retire from the early morning ordeal, but his phone decided otherwise. With a dejected look, he glanced at the phone.  But his face lit up with an unprecedented glitter when he saw the name. He picked up the phone with urgency and whispered, “Hold for a second.” He instructed Damian to leave him alone. After Damian left, with a gravelly voice he continued, “So tell me what you have found.”

Damian could see from a distance Benedikt’s animated conversation. He patiently waited and wondered what could be more important for Benedikt than to be in Lisbon, particularly knowing Sanchez was already hours ahead of them. After fifteen minutes, Benedikt was finally done with his conversation. He was oozing a sense of relief, a mystery that Damian would never be able to solve.

**Chapter 22**

***5:00 AM 17th April 2014, Zurich, Switzerland***

The constant beeping of the GPS shook Maxwell out of his slumber. In his apprehension of Sanchez escaping unnoticed, he had slept miserably. And now when he tried ditching his repose, he felt as if he hadn’t slept at all. The monotonous hum of the transmitter was an indication that the car was on the move. This time Maxwell wasn’t in a hurry, for the transmitter he had implanted the day before would help him find them without any more speeding car chases. He prudently gauged his options and decided to give them five minutes’ lead before beginning the chase.  While trailing, he made sure to keep a safe distance, but within reach, to help recover from any untoward situation.

After about an hour of driving, he saw the signs to St. Gallen-Altenrhein Airport. He couldn’t resist a smile and admired their intelligence in picking this airport instead of Zurich. “The best way to avoid any intruders,” he murmured. It was about time to accelerate and be at the airport before them. He knew he had the advantage of leaving the car by the kerb, unlike them, as they had to go to the rental office before heading to the airport.

Soon he was by the ticketing area. His vigilant eyes were fixed on the main door, screening each person. Ten minutes into the wait, he saw Sanchez arriving with her companion. He put the hood on to ensure minimum exposure of his countenance and started following them. After walking for five minutes, he saw them entering the TAP Portugal ticketing queue. This time he wasn’t surprised. He knew they would be heading to Lisbon but wanted to ensure his hunch was accurate. He followed them to the queue. There was one passenger between him and Sanchez. It was the closest he had come to her since Ayr, a state of affairs which he accepted with great interest.

He saw them purchasing tickets from the counter. As soon as they disappeared from his sight, he approached the ticketing lady, showed his ID, looked at her badge and said in an urgent but firm tone, “Miss Keller, I need you to answer a few questions about the two individuals you were attending to a few minutes ago.”

Maxwell had taken the lady completely by surprise. She looked frightened. With a stuttering voice she said, “I have to get my supervisor.”

“Sure, but you need to hurry.”

Within a minute, the supervisor came rushing to the scene. Maxwell showed his ID and continued, “I need a few quick answers.”

“Sure, sir. How can I help you?” The supervisor was listening with absolute sincerity.

Without wasting any time, Maxwell asked, “There was a young couple who bought tickets from this counter a few minutes back. I want to know which flight they are on. Can you also confirm their names?”

The supervisor was nonplussed, and he was not amused. From his appearance, it was evident that he had never faced a situation like this before. Maxwell was losing patience. “Look, you are wasting time.”

The supervisor regained his composure. He looked at the lady at the counter and commanded, “Get him whatever he needs.”

The lady sprang into action. Her hands were moving fast on the keyboard, and staring at the screen she said, “Their names are Sanchez and Stephen Roberti. They are on flight TAP020 to Lisbon. The flight leaves in about forty-five minutes.”

Maxwell pulled out his credit card and gave it to the lady. “Put me on the same plane. What seat number are they in?”

“24 A and B.”

“Give me a seat a few rows ahead of them.”

As she worked on the tickets, Maxwell turned towards the supervisor. “I know you need to report the incident. Make sure no one comes looking for them. If anyone has any questions, send them to me.” He handed his visiting card to him and continued, “They aren’t criminals. I am chasing them for a different reason. Is that clear?”

The supervisor, though perplexed, showed his agreement with reluctance. Maxwell swiftly moved towards the security gate. He had enough time to get a good breakfast before boarding the plane, something he had been looking forward to since he woke up.

He cleared security in ten minutes. As soon as he was out of the security check, he put his hood back on, covering a good part of his face, to avoid any direct contact with Sanchez. And with watchful steps, he sauntered towards the gate. His eyes looked around the seating area but couldn’t locate them. He glanced sideways and found a coffee shop. His famished being won the battle against his inquisitive mind, and he decided to grab a quick bite before looking for them. At the entrance of the coffee shop, he saw them both sitting and enjoying their coffee. They were in some deep conversation and missed seeing Maxwell entering the store.

Maxwell immediately retraced his steps, turned around as fast as he could and without looking back he approached the seating area. The waiting area was sparsely filled, suggesting that there were few people going to Lisbon that morning. He found a good seat from where he could easily observe them. Though unwilling, he ditched the idea of satisfying his hunger, at least for the time being. He pulled out a book from his bag and pretended to read, as he closely watched them, engrossed in some intense dialogue.

Sanchez’s intricate, composed character deeply fascinated Maxwell. It was love at first sight for him when he saw her about two years back. Sanchez was always a mystery to him, an enigma that his soul always yearned for. She was reserved, quiet, and always exuded a sense of calmness. Many times he was on the edge, ready to express his love for her, but circumstances beyond his control stopped him from executing his plan. Every time he met her, he felt as if he was meeting her for the first time. Every time she had a new hue and a trace of abstruseness. And every time he was left with the desire to know her more than before. She was an alluring dream, an impulse that was enigmatic and arduous to behold.  Today was no different, and he was drawn to her the same way as he was when he saw her for the first time.

The boarding announcements screamed through the speakers, alerting Maxwell. He slowly came out of his reverie. The time was about right for him to get some breakfast as Stephen and Sanchez were getting in line to board the plane. He dashed to the coffee shop and ordered an egg muffin and an espresso to go. He stood by the coffee shop and patiently waited till all the passengers had boarded. Soon the boarding area was empty, and it was time for him to get on the plane. He put the hood over his head and swiftly moved to the boarding gate. Once inside the plane, he took his seat as fast as he could, taking extreme measures to avoid coming to their notice.

It was time to relish his breakfast. He was content and murmured, “When you are hungry, even a simple egg muffin becomes the most sumptuous meal.”  Once done, he pressed the flight attendant call button. A pretty-looking woman in her early twenties arrived. She enquired with a wide smile, “How can I help you?”

“I am going to sleep the entire flight. Make sure you don’t wake me unless we are on the ground and I am still sleeping.” Maxwell’s tone intended the pun, and he passed a gentle smile.

“Don’t worry. Our slogan says, ‘with arms wide open’, and this is true for any comfort for our esteemed guests like you. Have a pleasant, restful flight.”

Maxwell found a reason to smile. He thanked her and drifted off to sleep.

**Chapter 23**

***10:15 AM 17th April 2014, Zurich, Switzerland***

The jet was about to leave Zurich when Benedikt’s phone rang. From the sound of the ringtone, he knew it was a call from the higher-ups. He waved at Damian to leave the cabin. This was the second time in the last fifteen minutes Damian had been asked to leave. Unsure how he would avoid overhearing the conversation, he ended up in the bathroom.

Benedikt’s face turned pale as the person on the other side continued with a monologue. After listening for a while, he barged in. “I will get her this time. My people are waiting at the Lisbon airport. I should have an update for you in the next couple of hours.”

The person on the other side continued with a tight voice, “Benedikt, your time is running out. You have taken way too long to arrest the situation. I am asking you again, do you need help?”

“I have it under control.” It was hard not to notice the frustration in his gruff tone.

“This is your last chance. Otherwise I will be forced to put my men on the job, no exception. I hope it’s clear.” The firm tone meant business, and Benedikt was aware of it.

“Yes.” He sounded choked as he forced it out with a great deal of reluctance.

“Good. Also, any information on the other person?” The barrage of questions never seemed to end.

“If he is chasing her, he has to be on the plane. We will keep a close eye on every person who gets off. If he is on the plane, we will get him too. I will call you as soon as I have any further update.”

“Ok. I will be waiting,” and the line was cut.

Benedikt heaved a sigh of relief as he put the annoying phone away. He was irritated for sure, but was unable to discern the reason behind it. Was it because of his repeated failure to get hold of Sanchez or his errant seniors frustrating him? Whatever the reason, he was sure of one thing. Sanchez was driving him wild. His inability to get hold of her before the ‘Sub Rosa’ was a huge concern and was testing all his remaining forbearance. But despite all the commotion and exhaustion, his craving for Sanchez had only increased by the moment. “Gosh, am I in love? It can’t be,” he murmured with some hesitation.

She had made his life miserable. He had to leave the poker game in the middle, something he had waited for the entire year. She made him look bad in front of his seniors after an otherwise spotless career, and forced him to run around like a rookie. All these were good enough reasons for him to loathe her, but instead, his heart was aching for her even more. He hadn’t experienced such restlessness before. Every time their brief encounter crossed his mind, his urge to be with her increased many times over. He had so many questions, and the wait to talk to her was almost unbearable. He knew confronting her was the only way left to put his anxieties to rest.

He came out of his brooding and noticed Damian was not around. Then he remembered how he had waved him away. There were two places he could have gone, the cockpit or the bathroom. The cockpit was out of the question as the jet was in motion. He headed to the rear of the plane and knocked on the bathroom door, and Damian came out embarrassed and rueful.

Before the takeoff, he dialed one final number and enquired, “Are you at the airport?”

“Yes, I am. The flight is on time. I should have them by the time you land.”

“Good, see you soon. Don’t disappoint me.”

The jet left for the skies, and Benedikt went into a reverie. Sanchez was laced all over his heart, thoughts, and soul.

**Chapter 24**

***10:00 AM 17th April 2014, Lisbon, Portugal***

The Lisbon air traffic was busy welcoming the spring tourists to Portugal. The plane was circling the airspace for a long time, struggling to get clearance to land.

Sanchez was looking down from the window and enjoying the panoramic sight. The skies were clear and the mid-morning sun was shining with brilliance. She could see the row houses arranged like pearls on a string, appearing exquisite and serene from above.  The plane started descending. The landing was uneventful, and as it was taxiing on the tarmac, the flight attendant’s voice rattled the quiet cabin. Although Stephen was awake, he kept his eyes closed, trying to get whatever respite he could before their next tribulation, but the blasting speakers were not helping the cause. The plane came to a halt and the passengers started disembarking. Stephen and Sanchez got off the plane with great caution. Their vigilant eyes were looking at people with suspicion, attempting to spot any goons before they succeeded in tracing them.

The immigration lines were long, and it took them a while to clear the check. So far, they hadn’t encountered anything alarming.  They paced out of the check gates and started executing their plan. The Lisbon airport map was engrained in their heads. They knew where they were headed with fine precision. There were restrooms right outside the check gates, before the airport terminal receiving area. They took a left and headed straight to the washroom. They remained there for a good twenty minutes, and when they came out, they donned a different look. Sanchez’s head was covered with a khimar, a headscarf that concealed her face from her nose down. A black veil masked her forehead and a full-length black abaya graced her body. She appeared a little stout, mostly because of the extra padding of clothes she had worn. By her looks, anyone could mistake her for a middle-aged lady from an Arab nation. Stephen sported a moustache. His head was covered with a boonie hat and he wore a military uniform. He had the stripes of a cadete aluno, a cadet officer in the Portuguese army. Stephen was a well-built young man, and with the military clothes on him he looked like a recent recruit.

The entire car-chasing episode in Zurich had made Sanchez overcautious and paranoid. The plan to appear in disguise was Sanchez’s idea. She had worked on the plan the night before in Zurich to dodge anyone who might be after them upon their arrival in Lisbon. The Arab ethnic wear was already with her, as part of her alternate plan to escape from Copenhagen.  Besides, appearing in disguise was always part of the evasion strategy. They carried a few dresses and adequate makeup with them in case circumstances forced them to don a different look.

They took different paths and headed straight to the underground train station for a ride to the City Hall. As they passed the waiting area, Sanchez’s incisive eyes noticed a corpulent person at the receiving area who, with a few of his companions, was guarding the exit path. Their appearance and the manner in which they were scrutinising each passerby made Sanchez believe they were there for a purpose. Her paranoia had led her to wear a disguise, which came to her rescue. She went past them without raising any alarm. Stephen was ahead of her and he also succeeded in concealing his identity from the prying eyes of the ruffians.

Their plan was to meet by the entrance of the City Hall Building. They chose not to be in the same train compartment and got on the train without any mishap. Sanchez disembarked at the Santa Apolónia station. There were two stations near City Hall. Stephen’s plan was to disembark at the Gare do Oriente station, which was a little further away from the Apolónia station. Once off the train, Sanchez headed straight to the ladies’ room. She took off her Arabic attire and changed into a floral printed off-white skirt. The skirt was broad, spreading down to her ankles. It was a modern version of the traditional seven skirts that local Portuguese wore. The seven skirts represented the number seven, which had significance in Portuguese culture – mostly the seven days, the seven colours of the rainbow or the seven waves. Matching her skirt was a black headscarf. The white-laced blouse embellished her bosom, as she donned the look of a traditional Portuguese lass.

She left the ladies’ room and walked towards the City Hall. It was around noontime in Lisbon. The sunshine was warm enough for people to enjoy the weather. Although there was a hint of humidity, the breeze travelling from the nearby ocean brought a sense of freshness. Tourists were flocking around the monumental building, taking every chance to capture the moment with their cameras and smartphones. The street food stalls across the square were buzzing with people trying out the local delicacies. There was a sense of happiness and peace that consumed the region.

Throughout the walk, she was vigilant. Her peering eyes were looking for the slightest hint that could suggest danger. So far, she hadn’t found anything out of the ordinary, but her mind was fretful, thinking about the person she had seen at the airport.  She knew that to avoid any further tribulations they must leave Lisbon at the earliest possible time. Contemplating her choices, she sauntered across the street to a coffee shop. She sipped her coffee, kept a good eye on the surroundings and waited for her brother to arrive.

Stephen’s station was a little further away from the City Hall building. He disembarked about ten minutes after Sanchez and paced towards the City Hall building. The edifice was a must-see sight for tourists visiting Lisbon. The crowd around the monument helped Stephen to camouflage his appearance as his eyes looked for Sanchez. A tap on his shoulder startled him. He turned around and saw Sanchez standing behind him.

Sanchez greeted him with glee as she hugged Stephen. To passersby it appeared as if two lovers were meeting after a long time, the man returning from his duties to meet the love of his life. The charade was an important part of their plan, for if the goons were still hiding in stealth, they would not consider them a threat. After a few minutes of pretence, it was time for them to leave the place. Sanchez looked towards Stephen and asked in a low tone, “Did you notice the stout person wearing a checked shirt at the airport?”

Stephen was trying hard to match Sanchez’s pace. “Yeah! I noticed, but then I wasn’t alarmed as he was looking away from us. The disguise worked. Great job. After all, it was your idea to wear these fancy clothes.” He almost chuckled as he said those words.

“That isn’t the point – what I am worried about is that despite all our efforts, we still got traced. We have been lucky to escape them so far, but I am afraid if we carry on like this, we will soon run out of luck. We must escape this gridlock.” She was breathless while hurtling through the cobbled streets of Lisbon.

“I agree. We need to give this good consideration. We are making it way too easy for them to locate us. We are liberal in using our identities for buying airline and train tickets, and I am sure it is working against us. These criminals have access to all information and tracing us is an easy job for them. In my opinion, we need to find a way to travel without disclosing our identity.”

Sanchez looked around as she crossed the street and continued, “You have a point, but more on this later. Let us plan to be in Nazaré at the earliest, hopefully without any more trouble. Also, now that you have made your point, we should ditch the train.”

Stephen was famished. His desperation to have a good meal was beyond comprehension. The past few days were nothing but feet on the streets and grabbing whatever came their way. While he was eager to eat, he knew Sanchez was right.  With clear hesitation in his tone, he tried to put his point forward, “Should we eat lunch before we head towards Nazaré?”

Sanchez rolled her eyes. She stopped walking, and her hands came and rested on her hip. “Stephen! At any moment Benedikt’s accomplices could be here. Soon they will know we dodged them. The best possible escape path was the train, which from the airport only comes to the City Hall – eventually, they will come looking for us here. I know you are starved, but you have to wait for a few more hours. I am sorry.”

Stephen gave it a final try. “What if we go to the waterfront? There are so many restaurants there, and they could hardly find us.”

This time Sanchez had a smile on her face. “You are an ingenuous soul. I know they will not hunt for us in the eateries, but they will surely guard all the city exit points. Let us not waste time. We need to be out of here soon.”

Stephen lost the battle and with dropped shoulders followed Sanchez, disgruntled and hungry. Leaving Lisbon was the utmost priority for her, and while she knew Stephen was famished she turned a deaf ear to his request.

It took them another five minutes to reach the bus stand. Despite being popular with tourists, the place had all its signs in Portuguese. Fortunately for her, Portuguese was her third language in school. The next bus to Nazaré was in thirty minutes – something she had dreaded. Sanchez was nervous. She had a wild hunch that the ruffians would come looking for them at any moment. Thirty minutes was long enough for anyone to make up for lost time.

She was in deep perturbation, weighing her options to circumvent the situation, when a young boy interrupted her contemplations. With a sharp accent, he asked in Portuguese, “Quer transporte privado para a Nazaré?” (“Do you want private transport to Nazaré?”)

It appeared as if the boy was God sent. The situation presented Sanchez with a way out of her consternation. She tried not to look overexcited, as that might result in her getting overcharged. With great composure, she responded to the boy, “Depende. Quanto é que cobra?” (“It depends. How much would you charge?”)

The boy sensed that he might have gotten a customer and led Sanchez to the other side of the stand. There were a dozen private cars parked in that narrow, crowded corridor, and a man in his early thirties guarded a small booth by the entrance. The boy rushed into the booth, provided a brief commentary and directed him to Sanchez.

The man grinned, showing his tobacco-stained teeth. “200 euros. Muito barato.” (“200 euros. It is cheap.”)

Given the danger that was looming over their heads, it was a sweet deal, but she gave a gentle push to see if she could bring the price lower. “Muito caro. Só posso pagar 150 euros.” (“Too costly. I can only pay 150 euros.”)

The man pondered for a while and finally stated, “Então tem de partilhar a carrinha.” (“Then you have to share the van with others.”)

Sanchez was in no mood to take any risk – she made a counter offer of 170 euros and the deal was done.

Within five minutes they were on their way to Nazaré. Stephen was hungry and exhausted, but still didn’t fail to acknowledge Sanchez’s charisma and her ability to think on her feet. He knew if it weren’t for her, their fate would have been sealed a long time back.

**Chapter 25**

***10:00 AM 17th April 2014, Lisbon, Portugal***

Maxwell deplaned in haste. The morning rush of incoming planes was pouring in, and the immigration lines had grown into long serpentine queues. He approached the official row, which was reserved for diplomats and government officials. There were only two people ahead of him and he went past immigration in no time. Before leaving the secured zone, he gave a cursory glance at the people waiting to clear immigration. He found both Sanchez and Stephen waiting in one of the regular long lines. There were about fifteen to twenty people ahead of them, and by his estimate it would easily take them another thirty minutes to clear immigration. He headed straight to the main terminal. The Lisbon airport was bustling with travellers, mostly tourists. April was a great time for visitors, as the weather was ideal.

He looked around for any dubious person but found none.  As he walked across the waiting area, his wary eyes caught some unusual activity. He noticed a visibly obese man scanning the arriving passengers in a surreptitious manner. Though he was some distance away, he could tell the man resembled someone he knew from the past.
