To avoid any direct eye contact, he moved to the left and walked sideways so that he could be diagonally opposite him. There was a coffee shop with a long queue of people waiting to get their mid-morning dose of caffeine. He joined the line and slowly inched forward. As he came close to the counter, he managed to get a good look at the suspect. His apprehensions were accurate. His name was Francisco and he was a close accomplice of Benedikt. He also noticed a few of his men dispersed around, looking at every passerby with vigilant eyes. Each of them had a picture in their hand, which he was certain was of Sanchez.

Maxwell knew he had little time at his disposal to stop Sanchez from getting past the immigration line. He left the queue and started pacing towards the immigration area. It was gruelling to refrain from running, but he managed to maintain a casual walk to avoid attracting any undue attention. The entrance to immigration was to the left of the waiting area. As soon as he was out of the direct sight of the goons, he dashed with all his might. He was at the restricted zone in under a minute. So far, he hadn’t seen Sanchez. He was hoping she was either in the immigration area or waiting in the baggage area for the luggage. Regardless, the only thing that concerned him now was to stop them from going out to the arrivals. He knew it might defeat his plan, for he would have to reveal his presence to Sanchez. But that was the least of his worries, as for now keeping her out of Francisco’s trap was his utmost priority.

There was a young lady guarding the entrance to immigration. She saw a man running towards her. She sprang to her feet and took her gun out of her waist holster. There was enough commotion for passersby to take notice, and they watched with disbelief and consternation. Maxwell swiftly flashed his ID, had a quick word with her and went past the checkpoint. People were curious but soon faded away.

He headed through the baggage area to immigration.  The lines at the immigration counter were longer than before. His eyes scanned the lines and fixed on the queue where he had seen Sanchez and Stephen waiting not too long ago. His heart sank when he couldn’t see them anywhere. He scanned the remainder of the lines but couldn’t spot them there either. He ran his eyes over the place a few more times, but there was no trace of them. Partially convinced that they might have cleared immigration, he turned back to check the baggage area. He gave a quick glance at the display board and noted the belt number for their flight. He pushed his way through to the carousel. The bags were still pouring onto the belt and people were crowding the area looking for their belongings. He scanned the area a few times but failed to locate them. He was perplexed and uneasy. He took a few long strides to reach the waiting area again and was relieved to see Francisco still waiting for Sanchez to arrive.

“How is this possible? Have I missed seeing them?” he mumbled. He searched every outlet but failed to find any trace of them. He knew he couldn’t have missed seeing them unless they had been called upon for secondary checking. He decided to scan a second time. He paced back to the baggage area and checked every corner, but failed to spot them again.

He was bewildered. “How can they vanish? I was guarding the area like a hawk,” he mumbled. Convinced they weren’t in the baggage area, he dashed to the immigration zone. He performed a final thorough scan but failed to locate them again. His options were running out and he decided to make a formal inquiry.

He approached one of the immigration officers, showed his badge and stated, “The lives of two people who arrived from St. Gallen on TAP airlines this morning are in danger. I need your urgent help. Their names are Sanchez and Stephen Roberti. Can you please find out whether they went past immigration?”

The immigration officer wasn’t amused. “You have to talk to the chief officer. I don’t have the authority to provide what you need.”

Maxwell was exasperated. Unfortunately, he had no other option but to meet the chief officer. “Can you please call upon him promptly? It is a matter of life and death.”

“This way.” The officer showed the path and sauntered with no urgency towards a bank of elevators. Maxwell almost dashed but the officer refused to speed up. The elevator took them to the third floor. The officer instructed him to wait outside and went through the door to get the chief officer. It was an excruciating five-minute wait before the chief appeared. Maxwell wanted to confront them on their casual behaviour but couldn’t do much besides repressing his anger.

“Someone’s life is in danger. I want your help in getting Sanchez and Stephen Roberti’s information at the earliest.” He managed to control his rage, but his taut voice couldn’t hide his infuriation.

The chief officer said, “We have some rules here. Can I see your ID? I need to verify your identity first.”

Maxwell’s annoyance was crossing all limits. “You can do all your checks later. If something happens to these people, it will not be good for anyone.”

The chief officer looked at him in an indignant way and declared, “This area is under my jurisdiction, and I define the rules. Follow them or you can leave.”

Maxwell knew arguing with this man wouldn’t help. He pulled out his identification and gave it to him. Though he was frustrated, he had no option but to wait. The officer returned after five minutes, and this time said in a soft tone, “I apologise, but as you would appreciate, we need to follow the rules. Now, to your question, they cleared immigration about 20 minutes back. Hope this helps.”

Maxwell was flabbergasted. He thanked them and rushed back to the waiting area. Francisco and his accomplices were still looking for them. A sign of relief appeared on his face, and he knew Sanchez had outsmarted not only Benedikt’s goons but him as well. It had been an eventful morning, and before he could decide what to do next, he needed some respite from the mad rush. He grabbed a coffee, found a table and relished his drink. His plan was to see what Francisco would do next. Besides, he wouldn’t have missed the chance of seeing Francisco’s expression when he learned the girl he was waiting for had outwitted him on the land where he considered himself the undisputed king, at least until now.

**Chapter 26**

***2:30 PM 17th April 2014, Nazaré, Portugal***

The drive to Nazaré was uneventful.  Sanchez remained attentive throughout the ride, doubting every vehicle that trailed their car. Stephen was too famished to worry about any worldly distractions. Besides, he trusted his companion’s vigilance, and in a most nonchalant way went for a short siesta. They were in Nazaré by mid-afternoon. The driver dropped them at the town square.

“I know you are hungry, but you have to wait for some more time. I am sorry, but I cannot take any chances. Let us walk out of this place and get a taxi from the street.” Sanchez avoided any eye contact while making her point. Stephen was too tired to argue and followed her out to the street. The afternoon breeze was making the sunrays soothing. The vast white sand beaches stretching to the horizon made a breathtaking view. Tourists swarmed the streets, and everyone seemed to be in a blithe mood, but not the Robertos. They were in Nazaré for a purpose. The scintillating views and the bohemian atmosphere couldn’t distract them.

Nazaré being a tourist destination, taxis were in abundance. They hailed one and instructed the driver to head towards Alcobaça, which was in the northeastern part of Nazaré, and with deep anxiousness waited to exit the place.

It was a short trip. They got off near an upscale restaurant. The afternoon sun was still glowing, scattering its hue across the wide horizon. Stephen was famished, but not angry. He wanted to make sure Sanchez was aware of that. He forced a smile and whispered, “If it wasn’t for you, we would have been hungry and tortured.”

Sanchez couldn’t resist having a good laugh and approached the restaurant with a buoyant spirit. Despite the late lunch hour, the restaurant was buzzing with people, a clear sign of a healthy spring tourist season in this small town of Portugal. The hostess was a charming young girl, dressed in short jeans and a white shirt, which had Cabeço embossed on it.

Her eyes crinkled and with a beaming smile she asked, “Minha senhora, onde prefere sentar-se, dentro ou fora?” (“Where would you prefer to sit, inside or outside?”)

Sanchez smiled and responded in Portuguese, “Dentro seria ótimo. De preferência, uma mesa mais reservada.” (“Inside would be great. Preferably a table with some privacy.”)

“Com certeza. Faça favour de me acompanhar.” (“Sure. Please follow me.”) She led them inside.

They settled in a booth diagonally opposite the guest entrance. This gave Sanchez a good view of the people entering the restaurant, allowing her to watch for any intruder without inviting any undue attention. The ambiance of the restaurant was pleasant, well supported by its modern décor. Located on top of a hill, the restaurant had a picturesque view of the region. With the lush green hilltop covered with wildflowers and the vastness of the Atlantic Ocean, it was an elegant sight. The occasional goats and horses grazed the land down below. Inside the restaurant, there was a live band playing traditional Portuguese Fado music. Keeping the tradition of the Fado alive, a solo singer was well supported by two people with wire-strung acoustic guitars. Fado expresses sadness, but the version performed by the band hardly reflected any glumness. Indisputably, it was a modified version to meet the audience’s needs, but it was done tastefully, preserving the local heritage.

They ordered a bottle of Douro red, one of the best Portuguese dry wines. Stephen settled for an açorda de camarão and Sanchez ordered cod in olive oil. The place was famous for seafood, like any good restaurant in this ocean-facing village. It was nearly five when they concluded their meal, looking content. Sanchez was relieved, for no untoward event had occurred since they left the City Hall. She was convinced this time she had successfully evaded her pursuers. But despite her confidence, she didn’t let her guard down. Her eyes still searched the area with inquisitiveness and vigour.

It was time to ditch their fancy clothes. Both headed to the restroom and came back appearing like tourists. Sanchez was wearing white Capri pants, a flaxen coloured shirt, and a bandana. Stephen wore jeans and a polo T-Shirt. Both were wearing beach sandals. Soon they walked past the restaurant towards the main marketplace. The breeze made the environment much cooler and more pleasant. The streets were still crowded with tourists, mostly shopping or heading out for the nightlife.

Stephen’s close friend Jules was in Nazaré. As part of an exchange programme, Jules had been in Ayr for about six months. On one of his countryside exploration trips, he was in Stephen’s village, where both met and became good friends. He kept visiting Stephen during his stay in Ayr. Jules specialised in cartography. It was Stephen’s idea to take Jules’s help to decipher the map. Sanchez was sceptical in the beginning, but Stephen succeeded in convincing her. For he was certain Jules could keep secrets.

Stephen hadn’t got a chance to let Jules know about their arrival.  Jules was expecting them but was unsure when they would arrive. Sanchez had given strict instructions to keep the phones off. If Jules had tried calling them, he wouldn’t have succeeded. Stephen didn’t want to appear unannounced and decided to give him a call. He spotted a public phone booth and dialed the number. Soon Jules was on the line.  After exchanging pleasantries, Jules offered to pick them up. Stephen resisted but gave in to Jules’s insistence. Jules could not get away before half past eight and asked them to meet him then at a nearby pub.

For the first time that evening, Sanchez appeared a little comforted. While Stephen was on the phone, she strolled across the street to the wooden benches by the beach corridor. She sat on an empty bench and gazed blankly at the wide-open ocean. The crashing sound of the waves was an integral part of nature’s calmness, and she was lost in it.

Stephen walked out of the booth, and to his horror, he couldn’t locate Sanchez anywhere. He was perplexed, agitated and scared, all at the same time. Many untoward fears crossed his mind, and with every passing minute, he started to lose the plot. He frantically started to look around, and after about five minutes of searching, he found her sitting on a bench right across the street. He was relieved to see her, but his anger refused to subside. “I have been looking for you all over the place. You could have at least told me before coming here.” His tone was a shade below screaming.

Sanchez didn’t react and kept gazing at the vast openness. After a brief pause, she broke the silence. “Stephen, come sit beside me. This place is so pristine, so beautiful. You need to experience this.”

He looked away to observe what his sister was trying to explain. He was instantly drawn to the quietude that resided in the emptiness of the night. Unlike the streets, which were swarming with tourists, there was no visible soul near the water. The only noticeable sounds were the crashing waves and the occasional cries of the seagulls. He sat beside her and with a soft tone mentioned his conversation with Jules. The talks receded, and they kept to themselves as if agreeing to an unstated protocol to stay by the ocean for as long as time would permit.

**Chapter 27**

***11:30 AM 17th April 2014, Lisbon, Portugal***

Maxwell was on his second cup of coffee when he saw Francisco huddling with his team. From his frantic appearance, it was evident he was aware of Sanchez’s disappearance. Soon Francisco’s men spread out in different directions. A couple of them went towards the metro station, a few rushed to the arrival gate, and Francisco, with two of his accomplices, headed to the baggage area. It took him a while to convince the security officer before he got access to the restricted zone. After about twenty minutes he came back to the waiting area. He looked flummoxed and wandered like a lost soul. Maxwell couldn’t resist smiling and enjoyed every moment of Francisco’s despondency. Francisco spent a few more minutes in the waiting area running helter-skelter in his last-ditch attempt to locate Sanchez. He realised his worst fear was a reality now. Without further ado, he dashed to the departure gate. A black Mercedes van was waiting for him. Soon he disappeared behind the sliding door, and the van zoomed past with a screeching noise.

Maxwell hurried after Francisco, but with caution. He had pre-arranged a car to follow Sanchez, but ironically his plan had altered. He tapped a number on his phone and soon his car appeared at the departure gate. He took the front seat next to the driver and instructed him to go after the van. The man behind the wheel was a local police officer, and he traced the van’s path with utmost discretion.

The traffic at the airport was thinning down as most of the morning flights had landed. It was nearly noon and the sun was shining brightly in this westernmost capital of continental Europe. The wagon was crisscrossing the traffic, trying to find its way out from the airport terminal in the shortest possible timeframe. Their car was a couple of cars behind the van and trod its path without much urgency, for the van was big and clearly visible from a distance. After a few minutes of maneuvering, the wagon took the A12 highway ramp. Their car followed and got onto the motorway. Once on the highway, the van took the extreme left lane and drove at a high speed. The motorway was densely populated with vehicles, enough for Maxwell to stay hidden. The van kept cruising for twenty minutes before taking the exit towards highway N4 and soon entered the parking lot of the Montijo private terminal. The traffic at the entrance of the airport was sparse. Maxwell instructed the officer to head straight on the N4 to avoid any direct confrontation with Francisco.

He was confident of Francisco’s location and had the luxury of taking a loop before entering the airport. They drove ahead for some time before turning around to head towards the private airport terminal.  He was aware of Benedikt’s flying only private jets and was certain that Francisco was there to receive him. The officer took the car to the nearest gas station and got out.

Maxwell gave himself ten minutes before heading to the terminal. It was a small airport and the parking area could easily accommodate a few dozen cars. The airport had only one runway, which was surrounded by a barbed wire fence. As he turned into the terminal, he saw the van on the tarmac. He parked the car in the furthest lane, switched off the engine and waited.

**Chapter 28**

***11:30 AM 17th April 2014, Lisbon, Portugal***

Francisco was enraged. He couldn’t fathom how Sanchez had evaded him and remained in a state of disbelief. His earlier hunch had led him to presume she hadn’t boarded the plane. But when the immigration officer told him about her arrival, his dwindling hope vanished.

“How is this possible? I was right there and had checked every passing person!” His voice reflected deep frustration, and he refused to accept the situation. He knew they couldn’t have gone too far and the only way to salvage anything was by demonstrating agility.  He called upon his men and spread them out to every exit. Benedikt would arrive at Montijo airport in forty minutes. Though he was not required to receive him, he decided otherwise. He wanted to be the first person to give him the bad news. It was a tough decision. He dreaded facing Benedikt’s wrath, but he knew it was the right thing to do.

It was a short drive to Montijo airport. He took the van straight to the tarmac, parked at the edge of the runway and stormed out with urgency. The executive receiving area was a small glass building. He went through the swinging doors and approached the main reception area.  The place looked deserted. There was a lady at the reception desk. He gave her the plane’s tail number and enquired about the arrival time of Benedikt’s plane. The plane was in the vicinity and was about to land.  Before the receptionist could finish her sentence, Francisco was gone. He marched out of the door back to the tarmac area.

With every passing minute, he was morphing into a disgruntled being. “What would I tell him?” he mumbled. He knew upsetting Benedikt wasn’t good for his business. Benedikt had the power, the authority to dethrone him and make him insignificant. He was frightened thinking about the consequences.  The only way he could save himself was by finding the girl, provided she was still in Lisbon. He had put his entire workforce on the job. They were guarding the city exits, scanning information on all travel tickets bought in the past hour and checking all nearby hotels. So far there was no success. How he wished he could get hold of her before Benedikt’s arrival.

The jet landed with a screeching reverberation, making Francisco jump out of his skin. It touched down a few hundred metres away from him. He made some last-minute frantic calls with the hope of finding some answers, but it was nothing but disappointment. Knowing he could not better the situation, he got back into the van and instructed the driver to move closer to the area where the jet would soon be parked.

The jet came to a halt at its designated apron. Slowly the engine noise receded. The jet ladder unfolded and within a minute Benedikt appeared on it. Following him was Damian, carrying a small bag. Benedikt was sporting a dark pair of glasses, which concealed any trickle of emotion from prying eyes. The strong demeanour of Benedikt was making Francisco squirm, and beads of sweat rolled down his temple. He had no clue how to announce the news. How would he disclose that the girl he had been instructed to nab was on the loose, and worse, he had no idea of her whereabouts?

Benedikt came straight to the point, “Didn’t we plan to meet at our usual place? I hope you don’t have any bad news to announce.”

Francisco’s throat went dry and for some miserable long seconds, he failed to speak. With great difficulty, he found his lost voice. “Chief, we lost her. I was personally at the airport and looked for every passing passenger. I don’t know how we missed catching sight of her. I ….”

Benedikt interrupted him, and Francisco’s voice trailed into oblivion. He was gazing at the ground. He had no courage left to face his boss.

Benedikt’s rage was visible in his screeching voice. “I gave you one job, Francisco, and you screwed it up. I told you how important this was for me. A twenty-something girl slips from right under your nose and all you can offer is news of her disappearance. Shame on you, and you know what, I am better off without you useless baboons. You all are a bunch of inept people.”

Though Francisco’s speaking rights were curtailed, he still mustered some courage and said in a low tone, “I have my men by all the city exits, and if they are in Lisbon, they will be caught.”

Benedikt lost his temper. He threw his hands in the air and blurted, “I don’t want anything from you. Do me a favour, stay out of it. Will you?”

Benedikt pulled out his phone and dialed a number. The call was answered promptly, and he walked away conversing. Soon he was far away, and Francisco could barely hear a word.

He was left alone with Damian. With great effort, he managed to glance at Damian. Damian greeted him with a tight-lipped smile and broke the long silence between the two. “I know what happened was terrible, but if it may console you a bit, this girl has deceived all of us, including the chief. We have been on the run for the last three days and she has dodged us multiple times. I must tell you, she is no ordinary girl. She is well trained and knows what she is doing. I know the chief is angry now, but he will come around.” Damian squeezed Francisco’s shoulder as he finished his monologue. Francisco didn’t say anything, but gave a benign smile, appreciating his friend’s solicitude.

It took Benedikt some time to finish the conversation. He walked back to where Francisco and Damian were standing. This time he looked more composed than he had a few moments back. “Did your men find anything?” His voice was flat, and there was no trace of the frustration he had shown before.

Francisco responded in haste, “Nothing so far. The exits are guarded. They will call me as soon as they find something.”

Benedikt continued, “They must be in disguise. I am positive that’s how they escaped you. Tell your men to look under the skin. They wouldn’t look the way they appear in the photo.” Francisco realised Damian was right on both counts. Benedikt had calmed down, which was a big relief, and this girl was no ordinary girl. He stepped aside and called his men, instructing them in Portuguese about what his chief had told him.

Benedikt continued upon Francisco’s return, “I have laid out a plan. If she takes the bait, we may have a long and adventurous night. In the meantime, let us get some rest.”

They all jumped into the van and started their onward journey. Benedikt reclined his seat and tried to get some sleep. The creeping silence in the van was broken by the ringing tone of Benedikt’s phone. Benedikt came to complete attention. He was keenly listening without uttering a single word. The call went on for five minutes, and finally he said, “Give him some time. I am sure soon you will have some good news.”

He snapped the phone shut and lay back on the seat with the hope of getting some respite from the chaotic day.

**Chapter 29**

***8:30 PM 17th April 2014, Nazaré, Portugal***

Sanchez was lost in her thoughts, and so was Stephen. Draped in the shroud of silence, they searched for their own solitude. It was about time, and Stephen took the initiative to break the long lull. “Jules should be here any moment. Let us walk towards the pub.”

Sanchez smiled through her crinkled eyes and followed Stephen’s lead. It was a short walk to the pub. Sanchez was amazed to note the stark difference between the two locations. The ocean was so calm, whereas the pub was bustling with a raucous crowd.  Loud music was pouring out of the pub as people opened and closed the door, nothing unusual for a tourist location. On a normal day, she would have loved the ambiance, but the recent tribulations she had undergone demanded that she be more vigilant.

The wait wasn’t long. Sanchez saw a six-foot-tall figure approaching them from across the street. His unkempt dark hair was flowing as he walked, and his sun-kissed skin was glowing even in the night. He had a well-toned physique, evident from the biceps and chest bulging through the body-hugging t-shirt he wore. He shouted in joy from a distance in a full-bodied voice, which added to his Greek-like appearance.

He hugged Stephen tightly, exhibiting genuine warmth. Jules was meeting Sanchez for the first time, but that hardly constrained him from greeting her with the same enthusiasm as he showed Stephen. His loud, cordial demeanour took Sanchez by surprise, and it felt as if she was meeting someone she had known for a long time.

“Why is your phone not working? I tried it so many times without any success.” Jules put forward an innocent question as they sauntered towards the car.

Without giving anything away, Stephen said, “Well, our phones don’t have roaming and we wanted to keep our costs low.” They all grinned.

Jules continued, “I can get you a local number for real cheap, twenty euros for seven days. That way at least you can receive my calls.”

Sanchez finally spoke. “That seems like a great idea. Why didn’t it come to my mind before?”

Jules couldn’t resist blurting, “Irmã, that is because of Stephen’s influence. When you meet smart people like me, ideas start to flow.” They laughed their hearts out, and Sanchez knew for certain Jules could be trusted.

Throughout the drive, Jules kept them entertained. He chatted without stopping, sometimes blurting out silly jokes and at times becoming a tourist guide, giving details of the places they were passing. With him, there was never a dull moment. Jules’s house was about five kilometres from the main street of Nazaré, in a village called Valado dos Frades. Sanchez could see the vast paddy fields in the clear moonlight. As they drove along the narrow single-lane road, she could see the occasional farmhouses appearing far off from the road, confirmed by the distinct dim light, which stood out in the openness. The weather welcomed them to roll down the windows, and the warm wind was gently soothing their being. The place looked deserted, rightly so, as it was past nine PM and most of the families would have gone to bed. The monotonous hum of Jules’s car engine was occasionally given company by a passing vehicle. Towards the end of their journey, Sanchez shut her mind off, despite the constant chatting of her companions. She enjoyed the pristine calmness the village offered, which drew her away from her rueful trepidations. Besides, the two friends were meeting after two years and had a lot to share, something Sanchez could easily stay out of.

Jules took a left on a muddy track and veered off the main road towards his house. The road was bumpy, to say the least. Jules warned them to hold tight to whatever they could find. He drove for another five minutes, and with their bodies shaking with every jerk, it seemed the longest five minutes of their journey thus far. The house welcomed them with an open gateway. It had two distinct pillars, but no gate to guard the house. The driveway was several hundred feet long. They drove along it for a while before they could get a clear sight of the house.

The bright moonlight, coupled with the glare of the lonely bulb on the porch, gave a clear view of the small yet beautiful farmhouse. The tall walls were draped in layers of white stucco, complemented well by the contrasting red terracotta roof. A four-foot wall made of local stones, which surrounded a quarter of the house, retained the vernacular essence of the place. Sanchez was awestruck by the petite beauty as they picked up their bags and walked through the front door into the house.

The interiors of the house were kept simple, not crowded with furniture, and spotlessly clean. The house had a refreshing aura and was welcoming. The door opened to a well-lit living area.  There were a few windows in the room opening to the exterior. They were designed using bricks in a crisscross, diamond-shaped fashion and were painted blue. The weather permitted them to be open all season, though each had a plastic drape, mostly to keep the rain from coming inside. The living room opened to a kitchen to the far right. In the centre of the room was a wooden sitting area, arranged in two rows of two, with a table neatly fitting between the rows of chairs. There was a single light hanging from the centre of the ceiling, and the walls were graced with a few pieces of local Portuguese decor.

Jules lived with his mother. As they entered the house, he announced, “Mãe must be asleep. You will meet her in the morning. Are you guys hungry?”

Stephen smiled and told him that they had eaten late in town. Jules continued, “Care for a glass of local wine?”

“Thank you. We will retire for the night. We have a lot to accomplish tomorrow.” Sanchez tried to conclude the conversation and passed a close-mouthed smile.

Jules refrained from any further small talk and showed them their room. It was a small but well-kept room with two beds, a cupboard and an attached bathroom.

“We rent this place during tourist season. Fortunately, the couple living here left this morning. Have a good night and I will see you in the morning.”

It had been a long day, and soon they were fast asleep. For a welcome change, they weren’t disturbed by any nightmares. They were too tired to accommodate any dreams.

**Chapter 30**

***12:15 PM 17th April 2014, Montijo, Portugal***

The jet landed with a roaring sound. Maxwell had a clear sight of the plane from his car. The jet ladder unfolded and Benedikt climbed down to the open tarmac. Benedikt’s longtime security head, Damian, also walked out of the plane. “Looks like the entire team is here to find a girl,” Maxwell mumbled, and a wry smile crossed his lips.

He saw them breaking into a conversation, and soon Benedikt was throwing his hands in the air. “Perhaps Benedikt has been made aware of Sanchez’s disappearance,” he thought, and he couldn’t refrain from smiling, imagining the unpleasant chinwag they might be having. Though he could hear the raised voice, it was not loud enough to get any sense out of it. Benedikt’s anger was extreme. After a few minutes of heated dialogue followed by a phone call, they got into the van and headed out of the airport. It was time for Maxwell to commence another car chase. He knew he had to be careful chasing these seasoned criminals. In the event he got caught, the consequences could be grave, for Francisco could commit any crime in Portugal and walk away unscathed.

The road connecting the airport and the main highway was a narrow two-lane road. There were no other vehicles on the road except the van. Maxwell had no choice but to wait, and soon the van drifted out of his sight.  After a few anxious minutes, Maxwell saw another car going in the same direction.  He drove right behind the other car, using it as a shield. The car in front was moving at a decent speed, helping him gain some of the lost ground. The road was a long stretch in the middle of an open field with no man-made or natural obstructions around. He was able to get a clear sight of the van even from a few kilometres behind. About ten minutes into the drive, he saw the van making a right turn. The sign on the side of the road indicated the turn would take them to highway A33. After the turn, the two-lane road became a four-lane road. Maxwell changed lanes and sped up slightly to make up the lost time. As they approached the highway, the road got busier, which he used to his advantage. He blended in with the traffic and followed the van with extreme caution.

Benedikt wasn’t in a hurry. There wasn’t even a shade of urgency in the way the van drove away from the airport. Soon the van took the ramp to enter A33 south. Maxwell followed them to the highway. He kept a comfortable distance from the van. After about 20 minutes, the van took the exit towards Vale da Amoreira. Soon they turned onto an unnamed street, or at least one with no visible name, and shortly after that, the van stopped in front of a dilapidated dwelling. With tall grasses and unruly weeds around, the house looked abandoned.

Maxwell avoided going into the lane. Instead, he drove a few hundred metres ahead and turned onto the next street. It was a narrow one-way lane squeezing between two tall buildings. As he drove a few metres forward, he saw a parking lot on the left, stretching north from where the building ended. There was a man guarding the gate. Maxwell took a parking ticket and walked back to where the van was stationed.

He found the van parked outside the house. The place appeared deserted. There wasn’t a single soul visible around the property. He stood at the corner of the street, hiding behind the walls of a three-storied building. He had a plan to execute but wanted to take some time before working on it. He monitored the house through the long afternoon and couldn’t find anything alarming. While the evening was slowly crawling in, there was still about an hour left for dusk to arrive. Maxwell was contemplating whether he should wait for the darkness to prevail or go ahead and get the job done. Undoubtedly, approaching the van in daylight was a risky affair, but the fear of Benedikt leaving the house was making him unsure. He weighed his options and decided to wait a little longer.

While waiting, Maxwell played the whole event back in his mind. It was puzzling for him to see Benedikt reposing when Sanchez was still on the loose. From what he knew about Benedikt, he wouldn’t relax until he had his hands on Sanchez. Either they had found her or there was a scheme that he wasn’t aware of. It was crucial for him to execute his plan, as it was the only way he would have any chance to learn Benedikt’s motive. Maxwell waited for another thirty minutes. There was no change. The van stood where it was, and no one came out of the house either. The sun was spreading its hue across the horizon, indicating its plan to retire for the day. The shadows of the tall buildings were slowly covering the streets, spreading their greyness across the van and the house. It was dark enough, and he decided to move forward with his plan.

He came out of hiding and walked towards the house.  The street by and large was quiet, except for a few people walking by on their way home from work. Soon he joined the sparse pedestrian traffic and reached the house in a few hundred steps. He had a good look at the worn-out dwelling as he went past it to the next intersection.  The house was at the end of the street, which allowed him to wait around the corner for the right time to launch his plan. He checked his bag to ensure he had the transmitter with him. He turned it on and made sure it was functioning.  The transmitter also had a built-in microphone, which could intercept the voices and relay them back. For that, it needed to be installed inside the van, which wasn’t an easy job. Though he had a set of keys, he wasn’t sure how long it would take to open the van door. He put the gadgets back into his small backpack, took a final look at the street and started walking towards the van.

The van was parked on the left side of the house. The porch was away from the house entrance. Maxwell knew that if he were on the passenger side of the van, people in the house wouldn’t be able to see him unless they came really close. The van was tall enough to hide him from any prying eyes. He looked both ways along the street and saw a person walking towards the van from the other side of the lane. He wasn’t worried, as he was facing away from the street, hiding from the direct view of the pathway. He grabbed the passenger side door handle and worked on opening it. He tried the first key, but the door stood like a piece of steel. He tried the second one, but it refused to give way. The person on the other side was fast approaching. He had time to check one more key before the person went past the house.

He was about to try the third key when he heard a creaking sound as if someone was opening a door. Before he could react, he heard footsteps approaching the van. He had a split second to react, and the only thing he could manage was to slip under the van. The van had good ground clearance, and he clung to the exhaust pipe, holding his breath. The engine had been shut off for some time, and the exhaust pipe was at a bearable temperature. As he wrapped himself around the exhaust, he noticed his bag dangling low from his back. Anyone with a good pair of eyes could easily spot the bag from a distance.

Before he could do anything, he saw two people approaching the driver side door. He could see their feet a few metres away from him. He wanted to avoid anything untoward and decided to get the bag off his back. While keeping his leg looped around the exhaust, he let go with one hand and slowly slid one of the backpack straps down. The strap came off that arm after a few tries. Half of the bag was touching the ground, and the other half was dangling from the other shoulder. With extreme quietness, he repeated it with the other arm, and the bag slowly came to the ground. He flicked it and kept it on top of him, above the exhaust, with his hands wrapped around it.

The men were talking in Portuguese. Maxwell had no clue what they were discussing, but he could make out that they were having a serious conversation. Soon he heard the unlocking beep of the van. They opened the front door. It appeared they were looking for something. One of them was still standing outside the van. Maxwell was relieved to note they weren’t taking the van away. After a few minutes they locked the van and started to walk back to the house.

A loud crashing sound made Maxwell’s heart skip a few beats. He saw a bunch of documents falling around the van. It wasn’t what Maxwell wanted, but at the same time he was glad that he had got his bag out of the way. They bent down and started collecting the scattered documents. Fortunately for Maxwell, they didn’t spot him. They gathered the papers in haste and hurried back inside.

Maxwell was relieved to see them going. He was dripping with sweat from head to toe. He wasn’t sure whether he was perspiring because of the hot weather or the commotion he had gone through.  He hung in his position for a few more minutes, and when everything seemed back to normal, he slipped down from the pipe and found his way back to the passenger side door.

He was shocked to see the bunch of keys hanging from the lock of the van door. His frustration was extreme. “How could I do this?” he mumbled. Without getting too distracted by his mishap, he went back to his job. The evening shadow crowded the space, and there was hardly any movement on the street either. After about seven tries, he finally heard a snapping sound, and the door gave in to his persistence.  He put the transmitter underneath the glove box in the lowermost corner. He checked it twice and ensured it was out of sight. Satisfied with his work, he swiftly came out of the van, locked the door and walked out of the area as fast as humanly possible without raising any alarms.

The evening was closing in fast, and Maxwell had an intuition that it would be a long night. He was content with his achievement and was eager to have Benedikt and his accomplice back in the van so that he could eavesdrop on their plan and accordingly decide on his next course of action. Once he was back in his car, he turned the receiver on. It was giving a blank tone and a constant GPS position of the house. He was happy to see the gadget working.

He had one more chore to take care of before he could finally get some respite. He dialed the number of the police officer who had met him at the airport and helped him with the car. After a few rings, the officer answered. The two men spoke for about five minutes. They concluded their conversation with an agreement to talk again in about an hour. Maxwell decided to get dinner. There was an Asian joint right by the street where he was parked. He entered the restaurant with the hope that the transmitter wouldn’t come to life, at least for the next hour.

**Chapter 31**

***8:00 AM 18th April 2014, Valado dos Frades, Portugal***

The village church bell rang with a loud peal, ruffling the quietness of the morning. The bell chimed with persistence, waking Sanchez from her deep slumber. It took her a few minutes to comprehend her location. The curtains on the windows were drawn, making the room pitch dark. She took her time adjusting to the darkness. Once she got the lay of the room, she reached for the table light and turned it on. The ringing of the bells had no effect on Stephen, and he remained in repose. She regretted losing time sleeping, something she couldn’t afford, knowing their lives were in constant peril. Deserting her morning laziness, she opened the curtains and the room was filled with bright sunlight. She was about to turn around when the view from the window caught her attention. She had never seen such a panoramic sight before. The house was on top of a hill, and from the window all she could see was grape vines in vast openness. The thin line of the shore far below appeared as if it met the horizon. There was the occasional chirping of birds, adding life to the serene abode. On the left side of the valley was a sporadic spread of old Portuguese style houses in no order, all of them draped in some distinct shade of white with red brick roofs. Down in the valley, she saw the church bell tower standing tall in peace and calmness.

The vast exquisite spectacle froze Sanchez, and she forgot the promise she had made to herself not to waste time. She was nudged out of her reverie by Stephen’s gentle tap on her shoulder. It seemed the morning glare piercing through the window had eventually stirred him from his sleep. Before he could say anything, he saw the welcoming sight of nature’s ingenuity, and like Sanchez, he too stood there spellbound.

“Let us get freshened up. We have a lot to do,” Sanchez announced as she took her eyes away from the view and headed towards the bathroom.

Soon they were in the living room. Jules was sitting at the small dinette in the kitchen area, chit-chatting with his mother in Portuguese. Jules’s mother appeared to be in her late fifties. She was a short woman, stockily built, wearing a traditional bright coloured bouffant long skirt and a shining red kerchief.

“Come to the kitchen,” Jules shouted as he saw them in the living room.

Jules’s mother had a broad grin on her face. “Dormiram bem?” (“Did you sleep well?”) Sanchez felt the warmth in the lady’s voice. She responded in Portuguese that they had a good night’s sleep. Jules’s mother was delighted to learn Sanchez could converse in Portuguese and invited them to sit at the small dinette. She put a lavish spread on the table. There was coffee, toast, fruit, boiled eggs and the traditional “sopas de cavalo cansado”, red wine with old breadcrumbs. According to the lady, it provided great strength to face the day. They devoured the sumptuous meal. It had been a while since they had a home cooked meal, and they enjoyed it wholeheartedly.  It was time to execute their plan.

Jules’s study looked like the den of a historian. The walls were covered with maps from all spheres. The room had one desk, which had a small display screen connected to a desktop computer. On one side of the desk, a Mac was running some CAD software with a European political 3D map. Right next to the desk was a couch, occupied with various instruments a professional cartographer uses – plotters, beams, large rulers, magnifying glasses and other drawing and engraving instruments. Jules was known for his work in Portugal. He also worked as a historiographer for the local Discovery channel.

They had to move things around to find space to sit. Sanchez brought her phone to life and showed Jules the map pictures.  Jules looked at the images with keen interest. After spending some time observing the maps, he connected the phone to the computer. The enlarged images of the map appeared on the large screen. He searched for a pen and paper in the pile of books and other materials scattered around the room. After a determined hunt, he found what he was looking for. And soon he was in business, lost in his own world. He scribbled some notes as he observed the map.

For the next hour, he remained engrossed in his work. He switched between the map image that Sanchez had provided and some of his own maps on the computer, all this while scribbling in a relentless manner on the notepad. They watched the genius at work with awe and hope. After a while of gazing, referring to maps and deliberating, he said, “This map is cryptic. I believe I got most of it, but there are things you cannot figure out only by looking at the map.” He turned around, smiled and, for the first time since they had entered the room, looked away from the map. Both Sanchez and Stephen were anxious to find out what Jules had learned.

Jules waved at them, asking them to come closer. He spread his scribbled notebook sheet on the table and continued, “This is a secret pathway map, designed to work on a strict schedule and timeline.” Sanchez and Stephen had no clue what he was talking about. They gave him a patient hearing, hoping to get accustomed to the lingo of a cartographer.

Jules was too involved with his findings to notice the puzzled looks on his friends’ faces. He continued, “Do you see the small dots across the map – these are the places that someone will travel to. Now there is an interesting colour pattern on the dots.” There was excitement in his voice as he pointed to the map. They could see the colourful dots and unequivocally expressed agreement.

“I believe this indicates the day of the week. There are only seven colours and they repeat in a synchronised pattern. Whoever is starting the voyage starts three days from now, or it might be three months from now, when the days of the week match what is coded in the map. My hunch is inclining more towards three days from now.”

“How do you know that?” Stephen’s curiosity had no limits and his voice echoed the sentiment.

“I knew this was coming.”

He gave a mischievous smile and momentarily took his gaze away from the computer to look at Stephen.

“When you zoom in on the small dots, like this, you can see a number. The first dot had the number 21, which is exactly three days from now. If you follow the pattern, you will see the number either staying the same or changing. I took the average travel time by both sea and road and it kind of fits well with the pattern.”

Sanchez was glad she had listened to her brother and made the trip. Jules was on a roll. “Most likely the first part of the journey is by sea. You must be wondering how I can tell that with confidence. Well, if you look carefully at the first few places on the journey map, they are all major seaports.” He pointed to the map and continued, “The journey starts at Tallinn in Estonia. From Tallinn it heads to Ventspils in Latvia and then to Gdynia in Poland.”

By now Sanchez was captivated, listening to every piece of information Jules was stating.

“From there, the journey will likely continue by road. The next stop, Warsaw, is in the centre of Poland, surrounded by land. The trip ends in Belarus in a city called Vitebsk. If you know a little about Eastern European geography, you will notice that Vitebsk is close to Russia, and the border between Belarus and Russia is porous. Traffic can easily travel in and out without any border security check. So, my guess is Russia has an important part to play. That’s all I have.”

Both Sanchez and Stephen were astounded. They had looked at the map multiple times but couldn’t figure out a thing. It appeared as if Jules was reading a script, a book detailing all the facts.

“We need to destroy all the evidence except for the phone images. It will be a good idea to memorise the information. Let us take some time to go over it.” Sanchez looked at Stephen for his agreement.

Jules gave them a brief test, and then it was time to destroy the evidence. The photos on Jules’s machine were deleted and the papers he had scribbled on were shredded. They thanked Jules, hugged him and decided to leave. Jules was keen for them to stay for lunch, but he knew they had little time and a lot to accomplish. He gave them a ride to the main street and bid them goodbye.

Sanchez had one more job left before leaving Portugal. They grabbed a quick lunch from a street vendor before heading to the other side of Nazaré.

**Chapter 32**

***9:30 PM 17th April 2014, Lisbon, Portugal***

It was half-past nine when Maxwell walked out of the restaurant. The GPS showed no movement, confirming Benedikt hadn’t left the house yet. With the passing time, his perturbations were gradually rising. He looked at the receiver, irresolute and torn. Laced with doubts, he decided to check the house to ensure he wasn’t living on false hope, and drove through the street where Benedikt was sheltered. He was relieved to find the van still parked on the porch.  The house lights were on and he was certain Benedikt had no immediate plan to leave the place.

Even though Sanchez had evaded him at the airport, he wasn’t worried, for he knew where she was headed. The only reason he had decided to go after Francisco was to stay ahead of Benedikt’s game plan.  A while back he had sent his police officer friend to guard the place where he was expecting her. However, until that moment she was nowhere near that place. With Benedikt’s insouciant behaviour and no confirmed news on Sanchez’s location, Maxwell’s concerns were growing with every passing minute. He dreaded the thought of Sanchez trapped by Francisco’s men, and his only hope was Benedikt, for sooner or later, when Benedikt decided to leave, he could follow his lead. But Benedikt was still there, resting unruffled in the obscured dilapidated house.

He had no option but to wait. Taking some rest was the best way forward for him, and he decided to get a hotel for the night. He found a small motel a couple of miles north of where Benedikt’s house was located.  Keeping the transmitter’s receiver next to him, he retired for the night with the hope it would wake him the moment the van inched forward.

**Chapter 33**

***3:30 PM 18th April 2014, Nazaré, Portugal***

Sanchez was looking for a public phone. She now had a local cell number but refrained from using it.  She had enough problems to deal with and had no desire to add any more to her list of worries. After a few minutes of wayward walking, she spotted a telephone booth right across from a deserted bus stop.  She dialed a number and waited for a response.

A lady’s voice came to life. “Quem fala?” (“Who is speaking?”)

“Am I speaking with Adriana?”

“Sim.” (“Yes.”)

“I am Sanchez Roberti. I come with a reference from John Patterson. I am in Nazaré. Can I come and meet you?” She chose her words carefully in Portuguese.

The lady paused for a while and said in a flat tone, “Está bem. Venha cá.” (“Ok. Come over.”)

Sanchez was a little disconcerted, for the lady on the phone sounded a tad indifferent. The way John had portrayed Adriana, she had expected a much warmer response. With a hesitant mind, she walked out of the booth. Stephen sensed her anxiety and enquired, “Everything ok?”

She put her dangling lock behind her ear and with a thin smile said, “Nothing serious. She sounded impassive. I hope she doesn’t change her mind.”

Though Stephen was worried, he never wanted Sanchez to be aware of it, so he put on his famous grin. “Everything will be fine. Let me get a taxi.” Soon they were on their way to Adriana’s house.

The taxi applied sudden brakes to dodge a pothole, bringing Sanchez out of her brooding. Stephen asked the taxi driver about the remaining distance, and the driver announced they were only a few minutes away. The midafternoon sun was seeping through the scattered clouds, making the tall shadows of the trees disappear in patches. The place was sparsely populated. All the homes she had seen so far had outside walls made of wood, well supported by brick-built structures, covered with pale stucco. The uniform row of houses, the broad grape vine farms and the pristine weather – all made the village appear like a sketch out of a picture book.

Adriana’s house was no different from the ones she had seen in the settlement. It looked like a weather-beaten dwelling, standing alone at the farthest corner of the street. The house was surrounded by an old picket fence, part of which was broken, clearly unable to withstand an earlier storm. There was a half-broken wooden gate, which was standing tall like a check post, guarding the entrance of the house. The gate led to a mud lane lined with unkempt briers, growing awkwardly and giving the façade a dilapidated appearance. Shrubs and weeds covered the lawn, and it appeared no one had bothered trimming the yard for years.

Stephen paid the taxi driver, and they carefully trod their path towards the house. As they neared the door, they could see the structure of the house more clearly, and it wasn’t as old as it had appeared from a distance.  The walls had plenty of tall windows covered with Castilian wrought iron, giving the house an elegant appearance. A single panel wood door guarded the entrance. There was a metal knocker right at the centre of the door, serving as both a handle and a mechanical doorbell. Sanchez tapped the knocker and waited for Adriana to arrive. It seemed she was expecting visitors, for she answered promptly. The door made a loud creaking noise as if it had not been opened in a long time.

Adriana seemed starkly different from how she had sounded on the phone. She carried a warm welcoming smile as she appeared from behind the door. She was a petite woman and looked much younger than Sanchez had imagined. The house might have looked ruined, but not her. Her shoulder-length hair was well kept and tied in a single ponytail. The diamond studs on her ears were elegant, well complemented by the platinum necklace that graced her long neck. She was wearing a low-cut evening dress draped around a well-toned body. The makeup she was wearing was light, perfect for the occasion. The distinct fragrance of Miss Dior greeted their senses. It appeared as if she was waiting for her date instead of them.

She showed them the way to the living area. Unlike the tattered exterior, the interior was tastefully done.  The living room was small, with pale plaster on the walls, but it was roomy, as the fittings were few and sparsely placed. There was a sofa in the middle of the room and an antique Quaker chair towards the far left. A wooden table was placed in the centre of the sitting area with a few books and magazines neatly arranged. A long rod dangled from the ceiling, holding a bulb that lit the room. On the far-right side, hanging off the wall, was a large picture frame of a young couple, Adriana and her partner, captured maybe a couple of decades back.

Sanchez and Stephen settled on the sofa. Adriana went to the kitchen to get some water, offered it to them and sat across from the sofa on an ottoman. Sanchez took a sip from the glass and with a low voice initiated the conversation, “Thank you, Adriana, for accommodating us at such short notice. John said you have great knowledge of maps, especially the ones that come from TransPacific. We are in a rush and would greatly appreciate your help in deciphering the one we have with us.”

Sanchez paused as she pulled out her phone and started fiddling with it to bring up the map pictures. Adriana’s bright smile showed a shade of pale in the cast shadows of the light bulb. She was perplexed to see how Sanchez came straight to the subject without showing any inquisitiveness about that ill-fated night. She forced herself out of her brooding and smiled.

“I will be happy to. John is a dear friend and it will be a pleasure if I can offer any help.”

By now Sanchez had the map displayed on her phone, and she passed it to Adriana. Adriana looked at the picture with great interest, and after several minutes of close observation, she said, “I need to enlarge and print it.” Still glancing over the phone, lost in her world, she asked, “Do you have a place to stay for the night?”

Sanchez looked at her brother but failed to decipher his views. She went ahead and explained their plan to leave Portugal that night.

“I need time to go through this,” Adriana said without taking her attention away from the map.

“We haven’t booked any hotel yet. We have been running blind since morning. Besides, we want to avoid staying in a hotel, as we noticed some people looking for us at Lisbon Airport.  If we need to stay for the night, we will need your help in finding a place.” There was a clear hesitation in her voice, confirmed by the way she was playing with her wristwatch.

Adriana looked away from the phone, brought back her infectious smile and continued, “I live in this house alone. You can stay here tonight. Why don’t you settle down? In the meantime I will print the maps. I have food in the kitchen, nothing fancy, but good enough to make a decent dinner.”

Sanchez thanked Adriana for her kind gesture and told her that they had a good late afternoon lunch and dinner might not be necessary.

“You will not regret the food. I have made some Portuguese delicacies. Take the stairs up to the first floor. There are two rooms, each with an attached bathroom. And when you come back, help yourself to the drinks. They are all by the kitchen area.” Adriana went ahead with her business without waiting for their response.

Though Jules had done a great job decrypting the map, Sanchez wanted a second opinion. Besides, Adriana had dealt with these maps before, and she hoped to get some finer details in case Jules had missed any. They took the stairs to the first floor and decided to occupy only one room. Sanchez got busy unpacking, while Stephen used the restroom.

Thud! It sounded as if someone had fallen right outside the house. The room faced the back of the house. She rushed to the window and raised the curtains to get a clear view. She saw a person trying to climb up the drainpipe. Before she could react, she heard the front door open with a bang. She hurried to the bathroom and whispered, “Come out quick, looks like we have some visitors.”

**Chapter 34**

***4:15 PM 18th April 2014, Lisbon, Portugal***

“They are here!” the police officer announced.

“Fabulous. When did they come? Are they alone?”

“They arrived a few minutes back,” the officer said in a hushed voice.

“Good. Keep a close eye on them and if you see anything unusual, call me.”

Maxwell looked at the clock. It was quarter past four in the afternoon. He had been lazing around the motel for a while. It appeared Benedikt had forgotten Sanchez, for he refused to leave the house. He had been debating what he should do next when the transmitter came to life.

He had already paid the motel dues in anticipation of a situation like this, and without losing any time he rushed to the car. The receiver carried the distinct voice of Benedikt, “We need to be there as soon as possible.”

Something didn’t seem right to him. Benedikt sounded victorious, as if he had succeeded in unearthing Sanchez from her hideout. He knew he had to act with urgency to avoid any mishap. Without giving it a lot of deliberation, he instructed his police officer friend to get Sanchez out of the house as quickly as possible. He saw the van finding its way to Nazaré, confirming his apprehensions. “How did Benedikt find out about Adriana?” he mumbled. Other than following the van, he had no concrete plan to curtail Benedikt’s advances, so he followed the course.

He was perplexed. He couldn’t afford to ruin the last three years of his sedulous plan. And deep inside, he was more worried about Sanchez than anything else. While he was reasoning with his quandary, he noticed the receiver was in complete silence. It appeared unusual, for he knew the van was on the move.  He concluded either the people in the van were observing silence or something had gone wrong with the transmitter. With no better alternative, he went after Benedikt, despairing and angry.

He was fifteen minutes into following Benedikt when he realised he had not heard anything back from his police officer friend.  In the past, in such a situation, he would have given it some more time, but this time it involved Sanchez and he couldn’t hold his nerve any longer. He called the number. The phone kept ringing until he heard the long beep tone. He tried the number again and nothing changed – no one answered the phone. His agitation was at its peak. All he could hope was to get to Sanchez before Benedikt. He regretted his decision to let her out of his sight. He tried the number again. This time the phone line was cut right after the first ring. His heart sank. He was certain something wasn’t right. He tried the number again and this time it went directly to voicemail. He knew he had a mountainous task on his hands, and driving fast was the only resort he was left with.

Right after the van turned towards highway A12 to make the final dash to Adriana’s house, a strange thing happened. Instead of going straight down the road, the van looped around and headed towards Lisbon. He had to decide soon. He could either check on Sanchez’s safety or go after the van. His intuition was inclining mercilessly towards the possibility of Sanchez’s capture. He didn’t have much time to sanely play out his options, and in haste he decided to check Adriana’s house first. He trusted the GPS locator to help him trace the van later if needed. For now, Sanchez was his only priority.

From where he was, the distance to Adriana’s house was a little over an hour, and it appeared the longest drive he had ever taken in his life.

**Chapter 35**

***4:15 PM 18th April 2014, Lisbon, Portugal***

A late lunch was ordered from a nearby restaurant. They ate in complete silence. Besides the occasional clinking of forks, there was no babel despite a dozen people in the room.

Benedikt was lost in his own world, thinking about the conversation he had with Oskar. Oskar was methodical, rational and always impeccable in executing any given job. When he informed him about Sanchez planning to visit a house in Nazaré, Benedikt was certain it would happen. However, with the passing time, his worries were slowly creeping back. Too many things had gone wrong in the last few days, rattling every one of his beliefs. The loud tone of the phone jolted him out of his cogitation.

“We saw them entering the house!”

“We are on our way. Keep a good eye on them. Remember, no mistakes.” A sense of achievement was reflected in his booming voice.

“We need to go right now.”

Francisco had no clue where they were headed. Benedikt’s indifferent behaviour towards him was making him agitated. He knew his chief was working on a plan with complete discretion. And the fact that he was not part of it made him anxious and fidgety.

Francisco took the driver’s seat, pulled his gun out of his pocket and threw it into the glove compartment. Soon they were on their way. The occasional passing vehicle was the only sound one could hear, for the occupants had decided to maintain complete silence. About five minutes into the drive, Benedikt got a call. After a few minutes of listening, he announced, “Get them and meet me at the warehouse.”

They had driven for another ten minutes when Benedikt got a second call. The conversation didn’t last long and ended abruptly. He instructed Francisco to make a U-turn and drive towards Lisbon. Francisco kept his curiosity in check and followed the instructions without questioning his chief’s intent.

Benedikt knew this was his chance. He kept checking his watch every other minute. And for the first time since his arrival in Lisbon, he appeared anxious.

**Chapter 36**

***4:30 PM 18th April 2014, Nazaré, Portugal***

Stephen came out of the bathroom startled. He grabbed the shirt he had taken off not too long ago and followed Sanchez, who led the way, tiptoeing to the other side of the house. They took shelter in the room opposite to where they had been before. The room was facing the front side of the house.  She zeroed in on the window as her escape route and slowly opened it to arrest any squeaking sound. There was a drainpipe running from the roof to the ground next to the window. She pointed to the water pipe, expressing her intention, and took the lead.  As she slid down the pipe, a loud screaming voice got her attention. She ignored the voice and continued sliding down.  Stephen mustered some courage and followed her. The height was manageable. Even if he had fallen, it wouldn’t have killed him.

Adriana was in her reading room when she heard some noise at the door. She wasn’t expecting anyone. Then she recalled Sanchez mentioning some suspicious strangers at the airport. Before she could comprehend, two intruders were standing next to her in the living room.

“Como se atrevem a entrar na minha casa sem permissão?” (“How dare you enter my house without permission?”)

The goons showed no interest in her hollow threat.  Soon they were in the room where her guests had been a few moments ago. They saw their belongings, but Sanchez was nowhere to be found. They peeped out of the window and saw two of their partners in a scuffle with a man. Losing them was never an option. They searched the house for any trace of Sanchez but failed.

As they were running from room to room, one of the goons shouted: “Ali estão eles!” (“There they are!”). He saw them running across the field towards a brick building. They jumped from the window and went after them. After a few minutes of chasing, they were near them. One of them grabbed Sanchez’s hand from behind. Sanchez turned around in a jiffy, clutched the shoulder of his stretched arm and swung him to the ground by rolling her body under him. Before he could react, she gave an elbow jab under his chin, and he collapsed to the ground unconscious. His companion couldn’t believe what he saw. He could never have imagined a fragile-looking girl could be this strong. He kept his guard up and was overcautious as he approached her with a half kick. Sanchez stopped his kick halfway and gave him a full-blown thrust to his groin. He too lay flat on the ground next to his friend.

Stephen was too shocked to react. She held his hand and forced him to run. A loud gunshot rattled the late afternoon quietness. A voice screamed, “Stop, otherwise the next one will blow your head off.”

Sanchez turned around and saw the person holding a .45 caliber gun in his hand. She saw the grit on his face and knew he meant business. They had no option but to stop. Sanchez was shattered to pieces. Her mission got curtailed too fast, too soon.

**PART IV**

**Chapter 37**

***4:45 PM 18th April 2014, Nazaré, Portugal***

The man with the gun approached the girl with cautious steps. He had seen from a distance how the girl overpowered his friends without effort. He was in a state of disbelief. He couldn’t imagine in his wildest dreams how a fragile-looking girl could demonstrate such power and execute with such perfection.  During this entire hullabaloo, there was one thing he was certain about. He would not underestimate the girl anymore. His eyes were constantly watching them with his gun pointed at their chests.  He was within striking distance of them, but he wanted to ensure that his targets had no more tricks up their sleeves. Without taking his eyes off them, he called upon his partner to search them. He was hopeful that the girl would behave. It was bizarre that he was more worried about the girl than the man who accompanied her.

His partner went ahead with his guard up. He turned towards Sanchez first. She was in her sleepwear. The man carefully tapped her pyjama pockets and screened her body. There was nothing on her. He refrained from taking any advantage of the situation. Once his partner had cleared her, the man holding the gun instructed her to turn around with her back to him. Next, his partner searched Stephen thoroughly.  Nothing was found on him either. Stephen was also made to turn around and was put about two feet away from Sanchez. Once done scrutinising, he turned towards his unconscious partners to see if he could bring them back to their senses. His attempt to revive them was interrupted by a loud ringtone. The phone belonged to one of the men who lay unconscious. With some hesitation, he searched for the phone. The tone was becoming almost unbearable as he fumbled to locate it. After some persistent looking, he found it in his friend’s trousers.  His face turned pale when he saw the name flashing on the phone’s screen.

“Who is it?” his partner asked in Portuguese.

He turned towards him and with a timid voice said, “It’s the chief,” and with hesitation pressed the answer button.

A harsh voice cracked through, “What took you so long?”

“Chief, we were in a situation...” Before he could finish, his voice was interrupted by Benedikt’s impatient scream.

“I told you to call me every ten minutes and it seems you didn’t bother. Do you have the girl?”

The man mustered some courage and with a trembling voice continued, “Chief, we have the girl, but we ran into a situation. The girl overpowered Paulo and Tiago, and they are lying unconscious. I am Nuno. This is Paulo’s phone. I answered as I saw you calling.”

“What the hell are you doing there when you already have the girl?”

Nuno’s voice was shaking with the onslaught of questions. He responded, “We are trying to bring them...”

Benedikt snapped before he could finish, “Now listen carefully. I don’t give a damn about your friends. Too bad, a girl managed to beat the hell out of you guys. Leave everything and bring the girl to me. Also, as I had instructed before, they shouldn’t be harmed. Is that clear?”

“Yes,” came the reply, and the line went silent.

Nuno had never spoken to Benedikt before, but he hardly regretted it now. His partner knew from his expression that it wasn’t a pleasant conversation. “We need to move quickly. Chief is waiting for us,” was all he could manage to say.

The man pointing the gun hesitated, but he knew they hadn’t been given any choice. Against their wishes, they left their unconscious friends stranded.

He commanded them to walk towards the house. Their car was parked a little away from the entrance. The walk was a few hundred metres through the open paddy fields. Sanchez was sensing the physical and mental trauma the two men were going through. She knew they weren’t fully focused on them, and their hearts were still longing for their friends’ wellbeing. She also knew this could be her only chance to break free. Had it not been for the gun, she could have easily taken them down, but for now she had to find some other way to escape the goons.

She gave a cursory glance at her brother in an attempt to get his attention, but he appeared too preoccupied to notice. She had no sight of the goons as they were walking behind her. Her senses were working on steroids and she had a vague idea that the man who was checking on their friends was close to her and his partner was guarding Stephen. She knew both possessed guns. How she wished Stephen could read her mind, but it appeared he had already surrendered to fate.  She looked ahead and saw the path curving to the left before entering the backyard of the house. She picked that curve as her spot to execute her plan. What she was thinking was dangerous, but she had to take her chances.

The bend was only a few metres away. Sanchez knew what she needed to do. She closed her eyes for a few seconds, drove all her unwanted fears away and focused on the fast-approaching curve. A few metres before the path curved, she increased her speed, and before anyone could comprehend, she bent low, turned 180 degrees and kicked the person behind her on the knee.

He was taken off-guard and screamed with pain. His gun flew from his hand and he held his knee in discomfort. Before the other person could grasp what had struck them, Sanchez wrestled him to the ground with a lock around his neck. She strangled him to the point he was breathless. He struggled for a few moments before he fell unconscious.

It took a few moments for Stephen to comprehend the situation. But soon he sprang to action and kicked the person who was guarding Sanchez in the chest with all his might. Already in agony because of the injured knee, the man couldn’t take the impact of the boot and collapsed to the ground. In a few minutes both the miscreants were lying senseless on the ground. Without wasting another minute, they ran towards Adriana’s house. They were worried about her and hoped to find her safe.

**Chapter 38**

***4:40 PM 18th April 2014, Lisbon, Portugal***

Damian had never seen Benedikt in such an agitated state. He demonstrated a teenager’s restlessness and the nervousness of a rookie. Damian had worked under him for over a decade, and never once had he seen him in such a wrecked state. He remained on edge, ready to implode at any moment. Though he refrained from mentioning whom he was after, it was quite evident he was chasing the girl.

Damian was equally frustrated about his failure to get hold of her. He was tired of the constant nagging and the rage Benedikt had embraced since the girl had escaped him at the hotel. Every passing moment was making him more unreasonable and unpredictable. He dreaded the thought of her leaving Lisbon unscathed, as that would make Benedikt obstinate, and he knew it would make his life a living hell.

Benedikt’s sudden screaming shook Damian out of his pondering. He saw him bellowing in frustration, failing to get someone on the phone. He called a few more times without any response and threw away the phone with extreme anger. “Take the next exit and loop around. We have to go back to Nazaré,” he announced in a stentorian tone.

“What has happened to him?” Damian brooded. Benedikt was known for his calm demeanour, never giving away anything in his expression. Damian could not recall a time he had raised his voice. There were situations which were tough, and circumstances that were life-threatening, but never once had he behaved in such an erratic manner. The only reason he could think of was the shame of getting defeated by a naïve girl. He could have accepted failure against a rogue, but a girl keeping him on the run and thwarting all his moves was making him wild, exposing his real self.

The phone lying on the floor started ringing. Benedikt fumbled to get to it, finally managed to pick it up and screamed, “Didn’t I instruct you to call me every ten minutes? What took you so long?” The conversation went on for a few minutes, and once he was done he announced, “We need to head back to Lisbon.”

By then they were already on their way to Nazaré. Francisco was clueless and frustrated, but all he could manage was a nod, and he swung into action following Benedikt’s order.

**Chapter 39**

***5:15 PM 18th April 2014, Nazaré, Portugal***

Adriana’s house was in complete shambles. The living room table was overturned, the chairs were upside down, and the floor was covered with muddy footsteps. It appeared as if the house had survived a colossal tempest.

Sanchez’s and Stephen’s eyes were searching for Adriana, but she was nowhere to be seen. Sanchez took the stairs to check the upstairs room while Stephen continued his search on the ground floor. The room they had stayed in not long ago was in total disarray. The place was ransacked and items from their bags were scattered all over the floor. Sanchez couldn’t find any trace of Adriana. All she found were traces of the rogues turning a beautiful house into a complete disaster. Once convinced of her absence on the first floor, she rushed down and found Stephen equally startled and shocked. Sanchez was puzzled by her mysterious disappearance. She knew Benedikt’s men were after her, and not finding Adriana wasn’t making any sense. Besides, all four intruders were lying unconscious in the backyard.

A sudden flash of wisdom crossed her mind, and she mumbled, “She must be in her workshop.” She looked at Stephen and asked, “Did you check the basement?”

Stephen looked blank and shook his head. And in unison, they sprinted to the basement.

This was the first time they had entered Adriana’s workshop, and it looked no different from what they had seen in Jules’s house. Scattered papers, unkempt piles of books, a dust-ridden computer and tons of cartographic instruments. She looked around and couldn’t find Adriana anywhere. There was a large velvet-covered sofa on the left of the entrance. The height of the sofa hid the area between it and the wall. Sanchez’s instinct drew her to the sofa, and she leaped over it.

There she was! Scared. Sitting in the corner, hiding behind the sofa. She bore no resemblance to the Adriana they had met a few hours ago. Her hair was all over her face, her clothes were crumpled, and her face had hues of terror. Her eyes appeared lost, staring into space. She had no visible mark of being hurt, the only good thing in the otherwise dishevelled surroundings. She was in a state of shock, and she hardly reacted to Sanchez’s sudden appearance.

Sanchez held her by the arms and enquired softly, “Are you ok?” There was no answer. Sanchez shook her hard to bring her out of her traumatised state. After a few moments, she came out of it, still shuddering with horror, and looked at Sanchez with disbelief.

“Oh! Thank God you are ok,” Adriana said. Her voice sounded brittle. She couldn’t hold back her emotions for long, and soon she bawled like a child.

Sanchez comforted her by holding her close and said softly, “We need to be out of this place at the earliest. They can come back anytime. Can you walk?”

Adriana confirmed she was fine to walk.

“Do you have a car?” Sanchez asked as she rushed out of the basement.

“Yes, I do. It is in the garage and the keys are on the kitchen table.”

The garage was a standalone unit, a few metres away from the door. Stephen led them as he sprinted to get the car out. He thrust the key into the ignition, and with a gentle nudge the engine revved to life with a loud roaring sound. He reversed the car onto the muddy road and waited for the ladies to get in.  Sanchez walked to the old Peugeot van with cautious steps, for she did not want to rush Adriana. She put Adriana in the back seat and rushed to join her brother in the front passenger seat.

A loud gunshot made Stephen jump, and his head hit the car roof. Before he could comprehend what had happened, he saw Sanchez collapsing in a pool of blood.

“SANCHEZ!” he shouted at the top of his lungs.
